A theory of transaction parallelism in blockchains
Massimo Bartoletti, Letterio Galletta, Maurizio Murgia

Abstract
. Decentralized blockchain platforms have enabled the secure exchange of crypto-assets without the intermediation of trusted authorities. To this purpose, these platforms rely on a peer-to-peer network of byzantine nodes, which collaboratively maintain an append-only ledger of transactions, called blockchain . Transactions represent the actions required by users, e.g. the transfer of some units of crypto-currency to another user, or the execution of a smart contract which distributes crypto-assets according to its internal logic. Part of the nodes of the peer-to-peer network compete to append transactions to the blockchain. To do so, they group the transactions sent by users into blocks , and update their view of the blockchain state by executing these transactions in the chosen order. Once a block of transactions is appended to the blockchain, the other nodes validate it, re-executing the transactions in the same order. The serial execution of transactions does not take advantage of the multi-core architecture of modern processors, so contributing to limit the throughput. In this paper we develop a theory of transaction parallelism for blockchains, which is based on static analysis of transactions and smart contracts. We illustrate how blockchain nodes can use our theory to parallelize the execution of transactions. Initial experiments on Ethereum show that our technique can improve the performance of nodes.

Volume 17, Issue 4, Paper 10

1.
Introduction

Decentralized blockchain platforms like Bitcoin and Ethereum
allow mutually untrusted users to create and exchange crypto-assets,
without resorting to trusted intermediaries.
These exchanges can be either
simple transfers of an asset from one user to another one,
or they can be the result of executing complex protocols,
called
smart contracts
.
All the actions performed by users
are recorded on a public data structure, called
blockchain
,
from which everyone can infer the amount of crypto-assets owned by each user.
The disintermediation stems from the fact that maintaining the blockchain
does not depend on trusted authorities: rather, this task
is collaboratively performed by a peer-to-peer network,
following a complex consensus protocol
which guarantees the consistency of the blockchain
also in the presence of (a minority of) adversaries in the network.

Users interact with the blockchain by sending
transactions
,
which may request direct transfers of crypto-assets,
or invoke smart contracts which in turn trigger transfers
according to the programmed logic.
The sequence of transactions on the blockchain determines,
besides the balance of each user, the state of each smart contract.
The nodes of the peer-to-peer network process the transactions sent by users,
playing either the role of
miner
or that of
validator
.
Miners group transactions into blocks,
execute them
serially
to determine the new blockchain state,
and append blocks to the blockchain.
Validators read blocks, and re-execute their transactions
to update their local view of the blockchain state.
To do this, validators process transactions exactly in the same order
in which they occur in the block,
since choosing a different order could potentially
result in inconsistencies between the nodes.

Executing transactions in a purely sequential fashion
is quite effective to ensure the consistency of the blockchain state,
but in the age of multi-core processors
it fails to properly exploit the computational capabilities of nodes.
By enabling miners and validators to concurrently execute transactions,
it would be possible to improve the efficiency and the throughput
of the blockchain.
Although there exist a few works that address this problem
(we discuss them in
Section

1.3
below),
their approach is eminently empirical,
and they are focussed only on Ethereum.
A comprehensive study of the theoretical foundations of transaction
parallelism in blockchains would improve the understanding of
these optimizations, and it would allow to extend them to other
blockchains beyond Ethereum.

1.1.
Contributions

This paper exploits techniques from concurrency theory to
provide a formal backbone for parallel execution of transactions
in blockchains.
More specifically, our main contributions can be summarised as follows:

•

We introduce a general model of blockchain platforms,
parameterized over the observables and the semantics of transactions
(
Section

2
).
Building upon it, we define the semantics of a blockchain
by iterating the semantics of its transactions:
this reflects the standard implementation of nodes,
where transactions are evaluated in sequence, without any concurrency.
We show that the two most widespread blockchain platforms,
i.e. Bitcoin and Ethereum, can be expressed as an instance
of this general model.

•

We introduce two notions of
swappability
of transactions
(
Section

3
).
The first one is extensional: two adjacent transactions can be swapped
if this preserves the blockchain state.
The second notion —
strong
swappability — is intensional:
two adjacent transactions can be swapped is the static approximations
of their read/written observables satisfy a simple condition,
inspired by Bernstein’s conditions for the parallel execution of processes.
Basically, these conditions require that the observables written
by a transaction are not read or written by the other transaction.

5
shows that the strong swappability relation
is included in the extensional relation.

9
shows that, if we
repeatedly exchange adjacent strongly swappable transactions,
the resulting blockchain is observationally equivalent to the original one.

•

For Bitcoin, we show
that the static approximations checked by the strong swappability
condition can be easily inferred by transactions:
the least approximations of the written observables
are the transaction inputs and outputs,
while those of the read observables are the transaction inputs
(Lemma
10
).
For Ethereum obtaining precise approximations is more complex,
because of its Turing-complete contract language.
We discuss in
Section

3.2
a few tricky cases,
and we report in
Section

5
our experience
with a novel tool to statically detect swappable Ethereum transactions.
We further show that, for both Bitcoin and Ethereum,
strong swappability is stricter then swappability
(Examples
3.1
and
3.2
).

•

Building upon strong swappability, we devise
a true concurrent model of transaction execution
(
Section

4
).
To this purpose, we transform a block of transactions into an

occurrence net
,
describing exactly the partial order induced by the swappability relation.
We model the concurrent executions of a blockchain
in terms of the
step firing sequences

(i.e. finite sequences of
sets
of transitions)
of the associated occurrence net.
In Theorem
16
we establish that
the concurrent executions are semantically equivalent to the serial one.

•

Finally, we describe how miners and validators can use our results to
parallelize transactions, exploiting their multi-core architecture
(
Section

5
).
An initial experimental validation of our technique on Ethereum,
which exploits a novel static analyser of Ethereum bytecode,
shows that there are margins to make it applicable in practice.

1.2.
Overview of the approach: ERC-721 tokens

We illustrate the main elements of our theory by considering an archetypal Ethereum smart contract, which implements a “non-fungible token”.
A non-fungible token represents a digital version of real-world assets, e.g. access keys, pieces of arts, and
serves as verifiable proof of authenticity and ownership within a blockchain network.
This kind of contracts are quite relevant:
currently, token transfers involve
\sim{50}\%
of the
transactions on the Ethereum blockchain
[
tok
]
,
with larger peaks due to popular contracts like Cryptokitties
[
You17
]
.

We sketch the implementation of the Token contract (the full code is in the Appendix),
using Solidity, the main high-level smart contract language in Ethereum.
This contract follows the standard ERC-721 interface
[
ESES
,
FFB19
]
and defines
functions to transfer tokens between users, and to delegate their trade to other users.

In Ethereum, a smart contract is similar to an object in an object-oriented
language: it has an internal state, and a set of functions to manipulate it.
Users and contracts are identified by their
addresses
.

The state of the contract Token is defined by the following mappings:

⬇

mapping
(
uint256

=>

address
)

owner
;

mapping
(
uint256

=>

bool
)

exists
;

mapping
(
address

=>

uint256
)

balance
;

mapping
(
address

=>

mapping

(
address

=>

bool
))

operatorApprovals
;

Tokens are uniquely identified by an integer value (of type uint256),
while users are identified by an address
(the address
0
denotes a dummy owner).
The mapping owner associates tokens to their owners’ addresses,
exists tells whether a token has been created or not,
and balance gives the number of tokens owned by each user.
The mapping operatorApprovals allows users to delegate
the transfer of their tokens to third parties.

The following function transferFrom transfers a token from the owner to another user:

⬇

1

function

transferFrom
(
address

from
,

address

to
,

uint256

id
)

external

{

2

require

(
exists
[
id
]

&&

from
==
owner
[
id
]

&&

from
!=
to

&&

to
!=
address
(0));

3

if

(
from
==
msg
.
sender

||

operatorApprovals
[
from
][
msg
.
sender
])

{

4

owner
[
id
]

=

to
;

5

balance
[
from
]

-=

1;

6

balance
[
to
]

+=

1;

7

}

8

}

The require assertion at line
2
rules out some undesirable cases,
e.g., if the token does not exist,
or it is not owned by the from user,
or the user attempts to transfer the token to himself.
Once all these checks are passed, the transfer succeeds if
the sender of the transaction owns the token, or if he has been delegated by the owner (line
3
).
The mappings owner and balance are updated as expected
(lines
4-6
).

The function setApprovalForAll delegates the transfers of all the tokens of the sender to the operator
when the boolean isApproved is true,
otherwise it revokes the delegation:

⬇

function

setApprovalForAll
(
address

operator
,

bool

isApproved
)

external

{

operatorApprovals
[
msg
.
sender
][
operator
]

=

isApproved
;

}

Users interact with contracts by sending transactions to the blockchain.
Transactions involve the execution of smart contract functions that may trigger contracts updates and transfer
of crypto-currency from the caller to the callee.
For example, consider a user (with address)
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
,
which owns two tokens identified by the integers
1
and
2
,
and consider the following transactions:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{}{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{Token}}}}:{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{transferFrom}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}},1})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{}{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{Token}}}}:{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{setApprovalForAll}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}},{\tt{true}}})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}\xrightarrow{}{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{Token}}}}:{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{transferFrom}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{Q}}},2})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}}\xrightarrow{}{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{Token}}}}:{{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{transferFrom}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}},1})

Intuitively, transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
means that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
(the sender)
calls the function
{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{transferFrom}}}
of the
{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{Token}}}
contract
to transfer the ownership of token
1
to user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}
.
Transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
delegates user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}
to manage
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
’s tokens.
Transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
says that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
transfers token
2
from
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{Q}}}
;

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
means that user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}
transfers token
1
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
.

Since each transaction modifies the internal state
of the contract
{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{Token}}}
, the order in which a miner
executes them is relevant.
For example, executing the sequence of transactions

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}

results in a state where
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
owns token
1
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{Q}}}
owns token
2
.
It is easy to see that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
can only succeed
if executed after
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
, because it depends on the fact that

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
is delegated by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
,
i.e.
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{operatorApprovals}}}[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}]
is
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{true}}}
.
Therefore, to run in parallel the transactions of
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
,
a miner would need to find an execution schedule
that does not affect the resulting state.
Our notion of
swappability
formalizes this intuition:
two transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{\prime}
are swappable
if they result in the same state, independently of their order
(Definition
3
).
For example, consider the transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
above:
regardless of whether
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
is executed before or after
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
, after their execution we obtain a state where token
1
is owned by
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
can act as delegate of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
.

Clearly, the notion of swappability outlined above is undecidable
whenever the contract language is Turing-equivalent,
like in the case of Ethereum.
Therefore, swappability cannot be directly used by a miner
to determine a parallel execution schedule.
We overcome this issue by resorting to a static analysis of the smart contract.
The underlying idea is to derive a syntactic approximation of swappability,
called
strong swappability
(Definition
3
).
This captures the fact that two transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{\prime}
depend and affect different portions of a contract state.
Thus, such transactions can be run in any order.
For example, the transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
above depend on and modify different parts of the state of the
{\tt{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{Token}}}
contract:
therefore, they are strongly swappable.

To detect if two transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{\prime}
are strongly swappable (in symbols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{\prime}
), one needs to statically over-approximate
the state variables that may be read and written during the execution
of the called functions.
They are strongly swappable if the set of variables written by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is disjoint from those written and read by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}^{\prime}
and vice versa.
This ensures that their executions are not interfering with each other.

From the code of transferFrom, we see that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
updates the
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{owner}}}
of token
1

and the
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
of addresses
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}

(lines
4-6
).
The variables read by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
are

{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{exists[1]}}}
,
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{owner[1]}}}
(line
2
),

{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{operatorApprovals[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}]}}}
(line
3
),

{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}]}}}
(line
5
), and

{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}]}}}
(line
6
).
Transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
updates
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{operatorApprovals[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}]}}}
.
Using the same reasoning for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
,
we obtain the following over-approximations of the
state variables written/read by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
–
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}

(we denote with
W^{i}
and
R^{i}
the variables written and read by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}}
, respectively):

\begin{array}[]{rcll}R^{1}&=&\{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{exists[1]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{owner[1]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{operatorApprovals[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}]}}}\}\\
W^{1}&=&\{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{owner[1]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}]}}}\}\\[4.0pt]
R^{2}&=&\emptyset\\
W^{2}&=&\{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{operatorApprovals[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}]}}}\}\\[4.0pt]
R^{3}&=&\{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{exists[2]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{owner[2]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{Q}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{operatorApprovals[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}]}}}\}\\
W^{3}&=&\{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{owner[2]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{Q}}}]}}}\}\\[4.0pt]
R^{4}&=&\{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{exists[1]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{owner[1]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{operatorApprovals[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}][{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}]}}}\}\\
W^{4}&=&\{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{owner[1]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{P}}}]}}},{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance[{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}]}}}\}\\
\end{array}

By the approximations above, we have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
, because
(R^{1}\cup W^{1})\cap W^{2}=\emptyset=(R^{2}\cup W^{2})\cap W^{1}
.
Similarly, it is straightforward to see that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
,
while the other combinations are
not
strongly swappable.

The strong swappability relation induces a partial order between transactions:
this can be exploited by a blockchain node to choose a parallel execution schedule.
To do that, from a given sequence of transactions,
we build an
occurrence net

[
BD87
]
,
a special kind of Petri net with no cycles and where places can hold
at most 1 mark.
This net encodes the partial order induced by the swappability relation and formalizes the concurrent semantics of transactions.
One of our main results is that
any concurrent execution in the occurrence net is equivalent to the serial one
(see item
3
of
16
).

Consider again the the sequence of transactions
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
above.
The associated occurrence net is displayed in
Figure

1
.
Intuitively, each transaction in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
corresponds, in the net,
to a transition (rendered as a box),
linked to two places (rendered as circles).
A transition can fire when all its incoming places contain the mark.
If two transactions are not strongly swappable, the corresponding
transitions are linked through a place.
In
Figure

1
, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
are not strongly swappable, the place between
\mathsf{t}_{1}
and
\mathsf{t}_{3}
ensures that
\mathsf{t}_{3}
can be executed only after
\mathsf{t}_{1}
, so rendering the dependency implicitly defined in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
.
The same holds for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
, and for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
.
Instead, transitions corresponding to strongly swappable transactions
can be fired concurrently.
In our example, this is the case for
\mathsf{t}_{1}
and
\mathsf{t}_{2}

(since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
), as well as for

\mathsf{t}_{3}
and
\mathsf{t}_{4}
(since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
).

Although in our example we have considered the tricky case where
the sender and the receiver of tokens overlap,
in practice this is a marginal case:
in Ethereum, the large majority of transactions in a block
either involve distinct users, or invoke distinct ERC-721 interfaces.
1
1

1

Although we are not aware of any work to support this claim,
some empirical evidence can be obtained by inspecting
the token-related transactions in
https://etherscan.io/tokentxns
,
which shows that this overlapping is a rare event in practice.

Therefore, we expect that in practice
the degree of concurrency of transferFrom transactions
is higher than shown above.

\mathsf{t}_{1}
\mathsf{t}_{2}
\mathsf{t}_{3}
\mathsf{t}_{4}

Figure 1
.
Occurrence net for
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
of the ERC-721 token.

1.3.
Related work

A few works study how to optimize the execution of transactions
on Ethereum, using dynamic techniques adopted from software transactional
memory.
In
[
DGHK17
,
DGHK18
]
, miners execute
a set of transactions speculatively in parallel,
using abstract locks and inverse logs to dynamically discover conflicts
and to recover from inconsistent states.
The obtained execution is guaranteed to be equivalent to a serial
execution of the same set of transactions.
The work
[
AKP+19
]
proposes a conceptually similar technique, but
based on optimistic software transactional memory.
The work
[
SH20
]
studies the effectiveness of
speculatively executing smart contracts in Ethereum.
After sampling past blocks of transactions (from July 2016 to December 2017),
the authors replay them by using a speculative execution engine,
and measure the speedup obtained by parallel execution.
The results show that simple speculative strategies
are enough to obtain non-negligible speed-ups.
Another observation of
[
SH20
]

is that many of the data conflicts
(i.e. concurrent read/write accesses to the same state location)
arise in periods of high traffic, and they are caused by a small number
of popular contracts, like e.g. ERC-20 and ERC-721 tokens.
The experiments in
[
DGHK17
]
suggest that parallelizing
transaction execution may lead to a significant improvement
of the performance of nodes:
the benchmarks on a selection of representative contracts
show an overall speedup of 1.33x for miners and 1.69x for validators,
using only three cores.

A main difference between these works and ours is that
they study empirical aspects of transaction parallelism
(e.g., the speedup obtained on a given benchmark),
while ours is more focussed on the theoretical counterpart.
Still, our theory is not intended to serve as a justification of
the correctness of the above-mentioned approaches.
Actually, we follow a different path to transaction parallelism,
based on static analysis of transactions, rather than on speculative execution.
The reason for this divergence lies in the fact
that optimizations based on speculative execution of transactions
are not fully compatible with current blockchain platforms.
Indeed, since speculative execution is non-deterministic,
miners need to communicate the chosen schedule of transactions
to validators, which otherwise cannot correctly validate the block.
This schedule must be embedded in the mined block:
since current blockchains do not support this kind of block metadata,
implementing in practice these approaches would require
a “soft-fork” of the blockchain.
Instead of performing dynamic checks, our approach relies
on a static analysis to detect potential conflicts.
Miners can use any static analysis to execute transactions in parallel;
once they have appended a block, validators just need to execute its
transactions, possibly exploiting another static analysis to parallelize
execution while preserving the semantics of the block.
In this way, our approach is compatible with any blockchain platform,
without requiring a soft-fork.

Our approach is based on static analyses of the variables read and written
by transactions.
Although the literature describes various static analyses of smart contracts,
most of them are focussed on finding security vulnerabilities
[
MCJ18
]
,
and they do not produce the approximations needed for our purposes.
A few papers propose static analyses of read/written variables,
but they are not specifically targeted to Ethereum bytecode contracts.
The recent work
[
PKS21
]
implements a static analysis
that approximates the portion of a contract state affected by
the execution of a transaction.
This analysis is then exploited to evaluate the parallel execution
of transactions over multiple
shards

[
LNZ+16
]
.
Although the commutativity relation inferred by the
analysis of
[
PKS21
]
is similar of our swappability relation,
it is not directly usable on arbitrary Ethereum contracts,
since the analysis of
[
PKS21
]
is targeted to contracts written in
the functional contract language Scilla
[
SNJ+19
]
.
Actually, the vast majority of transactions in Ethereum are sent to contracts
written in Solidity
(see footnote
7
in Section
5
),
hence this assumption could undermine the applicability of the analysis
of
[
PKS21
]
in the wild.
The work
[
DLP11
]
describes an analysis based on separation logic,
and applies it to resolve conflicts in the setting of
snapshot isolation for transactional memory in Java.
When a conflict is detected, the read/write sets are used to determine how the
code can be modified to resolve it.
The work
[
CCG08
]
presents a static analysis
of read and written locations in a C-like language with atomic sections,
and uses it to translate atomic sections into standard lock operations.
Designing precise static analyses for Solidity could perhaps take inspiration
from these works.

In the permissioned setting,
Hyperledger Fabric
[
ABB+18
]

natively supports transaction parallelism.
It follows the “execute first and then order” paradigm:
transactions are executed speculatively,
and then their ordering is checked for correctness
[
Fab
]
.
In this paradigm, appending a transaction requires a few steps.
First, a client proposes a transaction to a set of “endorsing” peers,
which simulate the transaction without updating the blockchain.
The output of the simulation includes
the state updates of the transaction execution,
and the sets of read/written keys.
These sets are then signed by the endorsing peers, and returned to the client,
which submits them to the “ordering” peers.
Ordering peers group transactions into blocks,
and send them to the “committing” peers, which validate them.
A block
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}}
is valid when
the keys read by transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}}

are not written by a transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{j}}
with
j<i
.
Finally, validated blocks are appended to the blockchain.

A preliminary version of this work was presented at
COORDINATION 2020
[
BGM20
]
.
The current version substantially extends it,
generalising the theory to arbitrary blockchain platforms
(while
[
BGM20
]
is focussed only on Ethereum).
Besides making it possible to extend our results to blockchains beyond Ethereum,
this generalization has allowed us to refine some of our results
to UTXO-based blockchains like Bitcoin.
The current work also contains the complete technical machinery,
including proofs, of our theory,
and the experimental validation of our optimization technique on Ethereum.

2.
An abstract model of blockchains

In this
section
we introduce a general model
of blockchain platforms, abstracting from the actual form of transactions,
from the language to write smart contracts,
and from the fact that transactions are grouped into blocks.
We then show how to instantiate this model
to the two most widespread blockchain platforms, i.e. Bitcoin and Ethereum.

{defi}

[Blockchain platform]
A
blockchain platform
is a tuple

({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}},\mathbb{O},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}},{\mathbb{\Sigma}},\sigma_{0},\mbox{$\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket$})
, where:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}
is a set of
transactions

(ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}},\ldots
);

•

\mathbb{O}
is a set of
observables

(ranged over by
{p},{q},\ldots
);

•

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}
is a set of
values

(ranged over by
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}},{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v^{\prime}}},\ldots
);

•

{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\subseteq\mathbb{O}\rightharpoonup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}
is a set of
valid blockchain states

(ranged over by
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\ldots
);

•

\sigma_{0}\in{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is the
initial state
;

•

\mbox{$\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket$}\in{\mathbb{\Sigma}}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{\Sigma}}
is the
state transition function
(we write
\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}
for
\llbracket{(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})}\rrbracket
).

The set of all finite sequences of transactions is denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}^{*}
,
and the empty sequence is denoted by
{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
.
The set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}^{*}
with the concatenation operator
and the neutral element
{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
is a monoid,
referred to as the
free monoid over
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}
.
A
blockchain

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
is an element of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}^{*}
.
The semantics of a blockchain
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
starting from a state
\sigma
,
denoted as
\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}
, is obtained by iterating
the semantics of its transactions:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma\qquad\qquad\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}$}\quad\text{where }\sigma^{\prime}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}

We write
\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket
for
\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{0}}
,
where
\sigma_{0}
is the initial state.
We say that a blockchain state
\sigma
is
reachable

if
\sigma=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket$}
for some
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
.

A
state update

\pi:\mathbb{O}\rightharpoonup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}
is a function
which defines how values associated with observables are modified.
We denote with
\{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}}}{{{p}}}\}

the state update which maps the observable
{p}
to the value
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}
.
Given a blockchain state
\sigma
and a state update
\pi
,
applying
\pi
to
\sigma
results in a blockchain state
\sigma\pi

such that, for all observables
{p}
:

(\sigma\pi){p}\;=\;\begin{cases}\pi{p}&\text{if ${p}\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi}$}\\
\sigma{p}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

We use
P,Q,\ldots
to range over sets of observables.

2.1.
Bitcoin

Bitcoin
[
Nak08
]
is the first crypto-currency based on
a decentralized ledger.
Its mechanism to transfer currency (the bitcoin,

B

)
is based on the
Unspent Transaction Output
(
UTXO
) model.
This means that each transaction spends the outputs generated
by one or more previous transactions,
and it creates new outputs, that can be spent by later transactions
according to programmable redeem conditions.
This model contrasts with the so-called
account-based
model,
implemented, e.g., by Ethereum,
where transactions update a global state,
recording the amount of crypto-currency in each account,
and updating the state of smart contracts.
By contrast, in Bitcoin the state is given by the
unspent transactions outputs, which
represent either

B

deposits redeemable by users
or the state of smart contracts
[
ABC+18
]
.
Although the language for specifying redeem conditions is quite basic,
complex smart contracts can be crafted by suitably chaining transactions

[
BZ18
]
.

We now formalise the basic functionality of Bitcoin
within our general blockchain model,
simplifying or omitting the parts that are irrelevant for our subsequent technical development.

Transactions

Bitcoin transactions are records with the following fields:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
is the list of
outputs
.
Each output is a record of the form
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}:{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}},{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf val}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}\}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}
is a
script
, and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}\geq 0

is the amount of bitcoins stored in the output.
Intuitively, a later transaction can
spend
the bitcoins
stored in a transaction output
by providing a
witness
which satisfies its script.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
is the list of
inputs
.
Each input is a pair
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},i)
,
meaning that the transaction wants to spend
the
i
-th output of the transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
;

•

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}
is the list of
witnesses
,
of the same length as
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
.
Intuitively, if the
j
-th input is
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},i)
,
then the
j
-th witness must make the
i
-th script
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
evaluate to true.
2
2

2

Bitcoin transactions can also impose time constraints on when they can be appended to the blockchain, or when they can be spent.
Since our theory is applied to parallelize transactions within the
same
block,
hence satisfying exactly the same time constraints, we omit them.

We let
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf f}
range over transaction fields,
and we denote with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf f}
the content of field
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf f}
of transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
We write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf f}(i)
for the
i
-th element of the sequence
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf f}
, when in range.
We interchangeably use the notation

({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},i)
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}(i)
for
transaction outputs
.
We use
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}},\ldots
to range over users,
and, we just write the name
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
of a user in place
of her public/private keys, e.g. we write
{\sf versig}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}})
for
{\sf versig}({pk_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}})
,
and
\mathit{sig}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})
for
\mathit{sig}_{sk_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})
.

Bitcoin scripts are small programs written in a non-Turing equivalent language.
Following
[
ABLZ18
]
,
we model them as terms with the following syntax:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}\;::=\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}

constant (integer or bitstring)

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}\circ{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}

operators
(\circ\in\{+,-,=,<\})

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle\mathsf{if}~{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}}~\mathsf{then}~{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}}~\mathsf{else}~{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}}

conditional

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}}.{n}

n
-th element of sequence
{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}
(
n\in\mathbb{N}
)

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle{\sf rtx}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}

witnesses of the redeeming tx

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle|{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}|

size (number of bytes)

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle{\sf H}({\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}})

hash

\displaystyle\;|\;

\displaystyle{\sf versig}({{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e^{\prime}}}}})

signature verification

Besides constants
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}
, basic arithmetic/logical operators, and conditionals,
scripts can access the elements of a sequence (
{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}}.{n}
),
and the sequence of witnesses of the redeeming transaction (
{\sf rtx}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}
);
further, they can compute the size
|{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}|
of a bitstring
and its hash
{\sf H}({\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}})
.
The script
{\sf versig}({{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e^{\prime}}}}})

evaluates to
1
if the signature resulting from the evaluation of
{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e^{\prime}}}}

is verified against the public key resulting from the evaluation of
{\color[rgb]{0.02,0.02,0.02}{{\it e}}}
,
and
0
otherwise.
For all signatures, the signed message is the redeeming transaction (except its witnesses).

The evaluation of scripts is defined as a function
\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},i}
,
which takes two additional parameters (used for signature verification):
the redeeming transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
, and
the index
i
of the redeeming input/witness.
The result of the semantics can be an integer or a bitstring.
The rules of the semantics are standard:
we refer to
[
ABLZ18
]
for a formalization.

{exa}

Consider a transaction of the form:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}

in
(1):
\varepsilon

wit
(1):
\varepsilon

out
(1):
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}:{\sf versig}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}},{{\sf rtx}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}}),{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf val}:80\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}}}\}

out
(2):
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}:{\sf versig}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}},{{\sf rtx}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}}),{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf val}:20\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}}}\}

The
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}
fields are empty,
making
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
a
coinbase
transaction
(i.e., the first transaction in the blockchain).
This transaction has two outputs:

({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},1)
allows
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
to redeem
80\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}}}
,
while
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},2)
allows
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
to redeem
20\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}}}
.
Assume that
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},1)
is unspent,
and that
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
wants to transfer
10\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
,
and keep the remaining
70\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}}}
.
To do this,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
can append to the blockchain a new transaction, e.g.:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}

in
(1):
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},1)

wit
(1):
\mathit{sig}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}})

out
(1):
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}:{\sf versig}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}},{{\sf rtx}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}}),{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf val}:70\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}}}\}

out
(2):
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}:{\sf versig}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}},{{\sf rtx}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}}),{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf val}:10\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}}}\}

The
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
field points to the first output of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
,
and the
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}
field contains
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
’s signature on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}

(but for the
{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}
field itself).
This witness makes the script
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},1).{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}
evaluate to true,
hence the redemption succeeds, and the output
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},1)
is
spent
.

Assume now that the outputs
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},2)
and
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},2)
are unspent.
Participant
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
can spend both of them by appending a new transaction

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
to the blockchain:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}

in
(1):
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},2)

in
(2):
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},2)

wit
(1):
\mathit{sig}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}})

wit
(2):
\mathit{sig}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}})

out
(1):
\{{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}:{\sf H}({\sf rtx}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit})=51,{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf val}:30\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to6.55133pt{\hfil\hskip-0.27748pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hskip 0.74pt\vrule height=1.1625pt,width=0.58122pt\hfil}}}\}

In this case, the recipient of the
30\textup{\vtop{\vbox{\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}\hbox{B}}\hbox to7.08336pt{\hfil\hskip-0.29999pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hskip 0.80002pt\vrule height=1.29167pt,width=0.6458pt\hfil}}}
is not explicitly
specified by the script
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}},1).{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}
:
actually, any transaction which provides as witness a preimage of
51 can spend that output.

Blockchain states

We define observables as transaction outputs
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},i)
,
and the set of values as
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}=\{0,1\}
.
In this way, blockchain states are partial functions

\sigma\in\mathbb{O}\rightharpoonup\{0,1\}
,
modelling the set of
unspent transaction outputs
(
\mathit{UTXO}{}
).
We denote with
U_{\sigma}
the set whose characteristic function is
\sigma
,
i.e.
U_{\sigma}=\sigma^{-1}\{1\}
.
Hereafter, when not ambiguous we treat
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
as a set,
rather than as a sequence, and we write
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
for the set of pairs

\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},1),\ldots,({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},n)\}
, where
n=|{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}|
.
The initial blockchain state is the UTXO
\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}\}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
is a coinbase transaction (i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}=\varepsilon
).

State transitions

We start by defining when a transaction is valid in a blockchain state.

{defi}

[Valid Bitcoin transactions]

We say that a transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is
valid
in a blockchain state
\sigma

(in symbols,
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
) when the following conditions hold:

(1)

({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}},j)\in U_{\sigma}
,
for each
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}},j)
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}

(2)

\mbox{$\llbracket{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}},j).{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf scr}}\rrbracket_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}},i}$}={\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}\neq 0
,
for each
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}},j)
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}

(3)

\sum_{{p}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}}{p}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf val}\;\geq\;\sum_{{q}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}}{q}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf val}

We say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is
consistent
when there exists some

\sigma
such that
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

Condition (
1
) requires that all the inputs
of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
are unspent in
\sigma
;
condition (
2
) asks that all the scripts
referred to by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
evaluate to true, using the witnesses
in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf wit}
;
condition (
3
) asks that
the value of the inputs of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is greater or equal
to the value of its outputs.

We now define the state transition function of Bitcoin as

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma^{\prime}
, where:

U_{\sigma^{\prime}}\;=\;\begin{cases}(U_{\sigma}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in})\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}&\text{if $\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}$}\\
U_{\sigma}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

We extend validity to blockchains by passing through their semantics,
i.e. we write
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
when
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket$}\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Further, we write
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}}
iff

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i-1}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}}
,
for all
i\leq n
.
3
3

3

The Bitcoin consensus protocol ensures that
each transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}}
in the blockchain is valid
with respect to the sequence of past transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i-1}}
.
Since our model requires the state transition function to be total,
we make the operation of appending invalid transactions idempotent.

{exa}

Recall the transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}

from Example
2.1
,
and let
U_{\sigma_{0}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}\}
.
We have that
\sigma_{0}\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
,
and
\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{0}}

is the UTXO
\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},1),({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}},1)\}
.

2.2.
Ethereum

Ethereum
[
But13
]
is one of the most used platforms for smart contracts:
it actually implements a decentralized virtual machine
that runs contracts written in a Turing-complete bytecode language,
called EVM
[
Eth21
]
.
Abstractly, an Ethereum contract is similar to an object in an object-oriented
language:
it has an internal state, and a set of functions to manipulate it.
Each contract controls an amount of crypto-currency (the
ether
),
that it can exchange with other users and contracts.
Transactions trigger contracts updates,
which may possibly involve a transfer of
crypto-currency from the caller to the callee.

Users and contracts are identified by their
addresses
.
We use
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{D}}},\ldots
to range over contract addresses,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}},{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}},\ldots
for contract functions.
We denote with
Addr
the set of all
addresses

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{Y}}},\ldots
,
including both user and contract addresses.

Transactions

Ethereum transactions are terms of the form:

{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}}})

where
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
is the address of the caller,

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}
is the address of the called contract or user,

{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}
is the called function,

n
is the amount of
ether
transferred from
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}
, and

\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}}
is the sequence of actual parameters.
A contract has a finite set of functions, i.e. terms of the form

{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\{{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}}\}
,
where
{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}
is a function name,

\boldsymbol{x}
is the sequence of formal parameters (omitted when empty),
and
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}
is the function body.
The functions in a contract have distinct names.
We denote with
{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}})
the contract at address
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}
.
We abstract from the actual syntax of
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}
,
and we just assume that the semantics of function bodies is defined
(see e.g.
[
BGM19
,
CPZ19
,
JKL+20
]

for concrete instances of syntax and semantics of function bodies).
For uniformity, we assume that user addresses are associated with a
contract having exactly one function, which just skips.
In this way, the statement
{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}.\texttt{transfer}({n}){}{}
,
which transfers
n
currency units to user
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
,
can be rendered as a call to this function.

Blockchain states

Each Ethereum contract has a key-value store,
rendered as a partial function
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}\rightharpoonup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}

from values to values.
The elements in the domain of this function are also called
keys
.
The set of values
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}
includes basic types, e.g. integers and strings.
An observable is a term of the form
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}
,
i.e. a key in the key-value store at a given address.
The possible blockchain states are the partial functions

\sigma\in\mathbb{O}\rightharpoonup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}
such that:

•

for all addresses
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}
,
\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
is defined;

•

for all user addresses
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
,

\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}
is defined iff
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}={\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
.

The second constraint allows for a uniform treatment of users and contracts.
The initial state
\sigma_{0}
maps
each address
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}
to a balance
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}^{0}_{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}}\geq 0
,
while all the other keys are unbound.

State transitions

Let
Const
be a set of
constant names

x,y,\ldots
.
We denote with
\llbracket{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma,\rho}^{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}}
the semantics of
the statement
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}
.
This semantics is either a blockchain state
\sigma^{\prime}
,
or it is undefined (denoted by
\bot
).
The semantics is parameterised over a state
\sigma
, an address
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}

(the contract wherein
\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}
is evaluated),
and an
environment

\rho:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\textup{{Const}}}}\rightharpoonup{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{\mathbb{V}}}
,
used to evaluate the formal parameters
and the special names
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}
and
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}
.
These names represent, respectively, the caller of the function,
and the amount of
ether
transferred along with the call.
We postulate that
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}
and
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}
are not used as formal parameters.

We define the auxiliary operators
+
and
-
on blockchain states as follows:

\sigma\circ({\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}})\;=\;\sigma\{\nicefrac{{(\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}})\,\circ\,{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}}}\}

i.e.,
\sigma+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}
updates
\sigma

by increasing the
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}
currency units.

{defi}

[Valid Ethereum transactions]

A transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}}})

is
valid
in a blockchain state
\sigma

(in symbols,
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
) when the following conditions hold:

(1)

\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\geq{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}

(2)

if
{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\{{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}}\}\in{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}})
,
then
\mbox{$\llbracket{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}},\,\{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}}},\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}}},\nicefrac{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}}}}{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}}$}\neq\bot

We say that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is
consistent
when there exists

\sigma
such that
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

Condition (
1
) requires that

{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
’s balance is sufficient to transfer
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}

ether
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}
;
condition (
2
) asks that
the function call terminates in a non-error state.

We define the semantics of a transaction
in a blockchain state
\sigma
as follows:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}}})}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\begin{cases}\mbox{$\llbracket{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}},\,\{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}}},\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}}},\nicefrac{{\boldsymbol{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{v}}}}}{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}}$}&\text{if $\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}$ and ${{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({\boldsymbol{x}})\{{\mathord{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathit{S}}}}}\}\in{\Gamma}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}})$}\\
\sigma&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

If the transaction is valid, the updated state is the one resulting
from the execution of the function call.
Note that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}
units of currency are transferred to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{X}}}

before
starting to execute
{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}
,
and that the names
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}
and
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}
are bound, respectively,
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}
.
If the transaction is not valid,
i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
’s balance is not enough or the execution of
{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}
fails,
then the transaction does not alter the blockchain state.
Invalid transactions can actually occur in the Ethereum blockchain,
but they have no effect on the state of contracts:
so, our semantics makes them identities w.r.t. the append operation.

{exa}

Consider a contract at address
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}
which includes the following functions:

{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{0}}}({})\{{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}\}\qquad{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{1}}}({})\{{{\tt{if}}\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}=0}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}.\texttt{transfer}({1}){}{}}}\}\qquad{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}}({y})\{{{y}.\texttt{transfer}({{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}}){}{}}\}

The first function only sets the value of the key
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}
to
1
;
the second one transfers a unit of
ether
to address
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
when

{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}
is
0
;
the last one always sends a unit of
ether
to
y
.
Consider a blockchain
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
where:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{0}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{0}}}({})\hskip 30.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{1}}}({})\hskip 30.0pt{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}})

Let
\sigma_{0}
be a state such that
\sigma_{0}\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\geq 2
.
The semantics of
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
in
\sigma_{0}
is:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{0}}$}=\sigma_{0}\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:2+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1

where the semantics of the single transactions is:

\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{0}}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{0},\,\{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}}},\nicefrac{{0}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}}}\}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}$}=\sigma_{0}\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}=\sigma_{1}

\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\tt{if}}\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}=0}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}.\texttt{transfer}({1}){}{}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{1}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1,\,\{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}}}\}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}$}=\sigma_{1}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1=\sigma_{2}

\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{2}}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{y}.\texttt{transfer}({1}){}{}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{2}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1,\,\{\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}}{{y}},\nicefrac{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{value}}}}}\}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}$}=\sigma_{2}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:1

3.
Swapping transactions

We define two blockchain states to be
observationally equivalent

when they agree on the values associated to all observables.
The actual definition of equivalence is a bit more general,
allowing us to restrict the set
P
of observables over which
we require the agreement.

{defi}

[Observational equivalence]

For all
P\subseteq\mathbb{O}
,
we define

\sigma\sim_{P}\sigma^{\prime}

iff

\forall{p}\in P:\sigma{p}=\sigma^{\prime}{p}
.
We say that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are
observationally equivalent
,
in symbols
\sigma\sim\sigma^{\prime}
,
when
\sigma\sim_{P}\sigma^{\prime}
holds for all
P
.

The following lemma ensures that our notion of observational equivalence
is an equivalence relation,
and that it is preserved when we restrict the set of observables:

Lemma 1
.

For all
P,Q\subseteq\mathbb{O}
:

1

\sim_{P}
is an equivalence relation;

2

if
\sigma\sim_{P}\sigma^{\prime}
and
Q\subseteq P
,
then
\sigma\sim_{Q}\sigma^{\prime}
;

3

\sim\,=\,\sim_{\mathbb{O}}
.

We extend the relations above to blockchains,
by passing through their semantics.
For all
P
, we define

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{P}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}

iff

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}\sim_{P}\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}

holds for all reachable
\sigma

(note that all the definitions and results in this paper apply to reachable states,
since the unreachable ones do not represent actual blockchain executions).
We write
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
when
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{P}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
holds for all
P
.

A relation
\mathcal{R}\subseteq{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}^{*}\times{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}^{*}
is a
is a
congruence
(with respect to concatenation) if:

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\;\mathcal{R}\;{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}\implies\forall{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\,:\,{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\;\mathcal{R}\;{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}

The following lemma states that
\sim
is a congruence:
therefore, if
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
are observationally equivalent,
then we can replace
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
with
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}

in a larger blockchain, preserving its semantics.

Lemma 2
.

\sim
is a congruence relation.

We say that two transactions are
swappable
when
exchanging their order preserves observational equivalence.

{defi}

[Swappability]

We say that two transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
are
swappable
,
in symbols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\rightleftarrows{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
, when
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

The theory of trace languages originated from Mazurkiewicz’s works
[
Maz88
]

allows us to study observational equivalence under various swappability relations.
In general, given an alphabet
\Sigma
and
a symmetric and irreflexive relation
I\subseteq\Sigma\times\Sigma

(which models independence between two elements in
\Sigma
),
the Mazurkiewicz’s trace equivalence
\simeq_{I}
is
a congruence between words on
\Sigma
.
Intuitively, all the words in the same equivalence class of
\simeq_{I}

represent equivalent concurrent executions.
In our setting,
\Sigma
is the set of transactions,
and
I
will be instantiated with various swappability relations.
The fact that
\simeq_{I}
is a congruence will allow us to
replace a sequence of transactions with an equivalent one
within a blockchain.

{defi}

[Mazurkiewicz equivalence]

Let
I
be a symmetric and irreflexive relation on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}
.
The
Mazurkiewicz equivalence

\simeq_{I}

is the least congruence in the free monoid
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}^{*}
such that:

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}
:

\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\,I\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\simeq_{I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

To exemplify Definition
3
,
let
I=\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}),({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}})\}
.
The equivalence class of the word
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}

under the relation
\simeq_{I}
is

\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}},\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\}
.
Note that, starting from the word
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
,
the other words in its equivalence class can be obtained by
swapping adjacent occurrences of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
.
This reflects the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}

are assumed to be concurrent, as they are related by
I
.

Intuitively, all the words in the same equivalence class
(with respect to
\simeq_{I}
) represent equivalent executions.
This is made formal by Theorem
3
below, which
ensures that the Mazurkiewicz equivalence
constructed on the swappability relation
\rightleftarrows
is an observational equivalence.
Hence, we can transform a blockchain into an observationally equivalent one
by a finite number of swaps of adjacent swappable transactions.

Theorem 3
.

\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}\;\;\subseteq\;\;\sim
.

Note that the converse of Theorem
3
does not hold:
indeed,
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
requires that
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}

have the same length, while
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
may also hold for blockchains
of different lengths (e.g.,
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is a transaction
which does not alter the state).

Safe approximations of read/written observables

The relation
\rightleftarrows
is undecidable whenever the contract language
is Turing-equivalent, e.g., in the case of Ethereum.
When
\rightleftarrows
is undecidable, to detect swappable transactions
we can follow a static approach.
First, we over-approximate the set of observables read and written
by transactions (Definition
3
).
We then check a simple condition on these approximations
(Definition
3
) to detect if two transactions can be swapped.
Of course, the quality of the approximation is crucial to the
effectiveness of the approach.
In general, the coarser the approximation,
the stricter the induced swappability relation:
therefore, an overly coarse approximation would undermine the
parallelization of transactions.

In Definition
3
we state that
P
safely
approximates the observables
written
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

(in symbols,
{P}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
)
when executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
does not alter the state of the observables not in
P
.
Defining the set of
read
observables is a bit trickier:
we require that executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
in two states that agree
on the values of the observables in the read set
results in two states that differ at most on the
observables where they did not agree before the execution of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

{defi}

[Safe approximation of read/written observables]

Given a set of observables
P
and a transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
, we define:

\displaystyle{P}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}

iff

\displaystyle\forall Q:Q\cap P=\emptyset\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}

\displaystyle{P}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}

iff

\displaystyle\forall{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}},{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}},Q:{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{P}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}\land{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}\implies{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

{exa}

Recall from Example
2.2
the Ethereum transaction:
