{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}})\qquad\qquad\text{where }\;{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}}({y})\{{{y}.\texttt{transfer}({1}){}{}}\}

The execution of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
affects the
{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}
; however,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
is first incremented
and then decremented, so its value is unchanged.
Then,
\{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}.{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}.{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}}\}
is a safe approximation of the observables
written
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
, i.e.
{\{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}.{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}.{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}}\}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
.

A safe approximation of the observables
read
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}

is
P=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\}
.
To prove this, consider two blockchains
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
,
and a set of observables
Q
such that

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{P}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
.
We have two cases:

•

If
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}<1
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
is not valid in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
, and so

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket$}
.
Since
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{P}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
,
then
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}<1
,
so
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
is not valid also in
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
,
from which we have
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}
.

•

If
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}=n\geq 1
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
is valid in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}

affects exactly
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
,
as it transfers
1\,\textit{ether}
from
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
.
Since
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
,
the states of
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
may only
differ on
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
or
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
.
However:

\displaystyle\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}=n-1=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}+1=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}+1=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}

Therefore, in both cases
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
,
and so we have proved that
{\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
.

Widening a safe approximation (either of read or written observables)
preserves its safety;
further, the intersection
of two write approximations is still safe.
From this, it follows that there exists a
least
safe approximation
of the observables written by a transaction.

Lemma 4
.

Let
\bullet\in\{r,w\}
. Then:

(1)

if
{P}\models^{\bullet}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
and
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
,
then
{P^{\prime}}\models^{\bullet}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
;

(2)

if
{P}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}

and
{Q}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,
then
{P\cap Q}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.

The following example shows that, in general, part
2

of Lemma
4

does not hold for read approximations.

{exa}

Let
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}
be an Ethereum contract with functions:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({x})\;\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}}{\tt{:=}}{x};{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k^{\prime}}}}{\tt{:=}}{x}\}

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}({})\;\{{\tt{if}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}.\texttt{transfer}({{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}}}){}{}}\,{\tt{else}}\,{{\tt{skip}}}\}

and let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{0}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}({})
.
Note that, in any reachable state
\sigma
,
it must be
\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}=\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k^{\prime}}}
.
Let
Q
be such that
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
,
and let
\sigma=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket$}
,
\sigma^{\prime}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}
,
let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}=\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
, and
let
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n^{\prime}}}=\sigma^{\prime}\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
.
Appending
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
to
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
will result in:

\displaystyle\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\begin{cases}\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n}}&\text{if $\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}$}\\
\sigma&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\quad

\displaystyle\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}$}=\begin{cases}\sigma^{\prime}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n^{\prime}}}+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{n^{\prime}}}&\text{if $\sigma^{\prime}\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}$}\\
\sigma^{\prime}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}\}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
, then the conditions

\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
and
\sigma^{\prime}\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}

are equivalent.
Therefore,

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}\sim_{Q}\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket$}
,
and so we have proved that
{\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}\}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.
Similarly, we obtain that
{\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k^{\prime}}}\}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,
since
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}
and
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k^{\prime}}}
are always bound to the same value.
Note however that
\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}\}\cap\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k^{\prime}}}\}=\emptyset

is
not
a safe approximation of the observables read by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
For instance, if
\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}\neq\sigma^{\prime}\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k^{\prime}}}

and
\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}=\sigma^{\prime}\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
,
then appending
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
to
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
or to
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
results in states
which differ in the balance of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Strong swappability

We use safe approximations of read/written observables
to detect when two transactions are swappable,
recasting in our setting Bernstein’s conditions
[
Ber66
]

for the parallel execution of processes.
More specifically,
we require that the set of observables written by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

is disjoint from those written or read by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
, and vice versa.
When this happens, we say that the
two transactions are
strongly swappable
.

{defi}

[Strong swappability]

We say that two transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
are
strongly swappable
,
in symbols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
, when
there exist
W,W^{\prime},R,R^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbb{O}
such that

{W}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,

{W^{\prime}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}
,

{R}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,

{R^{\prime}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}
,
and:

\big(R\cup W\big)\cap W^{\prime}\;=\;\emptyset\;=\;\big(R^{\prime}\cup W^{\prime}\big)\cap W

The following theorem ensures the soundness of our approximation:
if two transactions are strongly swappable, then they are also swappable.
Since its proof depends on notions that have yet to be defined,
we postpone it at the end of the section.
The converse implication does not hold neither in Bitcoin nor in Ethereum,
as shown by Examples
3.1
and
3.2
.

Theorem 5
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\rightleftarrows{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
.

Theorem
6
states that
the Mazurkiewicz equivalence
\simeq_{\#}

is stricter than
\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}
.
Together with Theorem
3
,
if
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
is transformed into
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}

by exchanging adjacent strongly swappable transactions,
then
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
are observationally equivalent.

Theorem 6
.

\mathord{\simeq_{\#}}\subseteq\mathord{\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}}
.

Parameterised strong swappability

Note that if the contract language is Turing-equivalent,
then finding approximations which satisfy
the disjointness condition in Definition
3
is not computable,
and so the relation
\#
is undecidable.
This is because strong swappability abstracts from the actual static analysis
used to compute the safe approximations:
it just assumes that these approximations exist.
Definition
3
below parameterises strong swappability over
a static analysis, which we render as a function
from transactions to sets of observables.
Formally,
\mathtt{W}
is a
static analysis of written observables

when
{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
, for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
;
similarly,
\mathtt{R}
is a
static analysis of read observables

when
{\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
, for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

{defi}

[Parameterised strong swappability]

Let
\mathtt{W}
and
\mathtt{R}
be static analyses of written/read observables.
We say that two transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\neq{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
are

strongly swappable w.r.t.
\mathtt{W}
and
\mathtt{R}
,
in symbols
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
, if:

\big(\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\cup\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\big)\cap\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})\;=\;\emptyset\;=\;\big(\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})\cup\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})\big)\cap\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})

By the definition, it directly follows that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
implies that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
.
Further, if
\mathtt{W}
and
\mathtt{R}
are computable,
then
\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}
is decidable.
Later on, we will show that the relations
\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}
and
\#

are equivalent in Bitcoin (Theorem
13
).

Lemma 7
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

Proof 3.1
.

By Definition
3
,

\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\cap\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})=\emptyset
and

\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\cap\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})=\emptyset
.
Since
{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}
,
by Definition
3
we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
.
Since
\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}
is a congruence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Since
{\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
,
by Definition
3
we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
By simmetry and transitivity of
\sim
(Lemma
1
),
we conclude
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

The following lemma states that the relation
\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}

is a sound approximation of swappability.

Lemma 8
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\rightleftarrows{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}

Proof 3.2
.

By applying Lemma
7
twice, we obtain

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
.
Let
P=\mathbb{O}\setminus(\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\cup\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}))
.
Since
P\cap\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})=\emptyset=P\cap\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})
,
by applying Definition
3
twice we obtain

{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}\sim_{P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}\sim_{P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
.
Since
\sim_{P}
is a congruence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Summing up:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\sim_{P\,\cup\,(\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\cup\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}))}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}

from which we obtain the thesis, since

P\cup(\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\cup\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}))=\mathbb{O}
and

\sim_{\mathbb{O}}\,=\,\sim
.

Note that if
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
, then there exist

\mathtt{W}
and
\mathtt{R}
such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
.
Then, from Lemma
8
it follows
that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
are swappable.
This proves Theorem
5
,
from which in turns we obtain Theorem
6
.
Putting it all together, we have proved the inclusions:

Theorem 9
.

\simeq_{\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}}\;\;\subseteq\;\;\simeq_{\#}\;\;\subseteq\;\;\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}
.

3.1.
Swapping transactions in Bitcoin

By instantiating our general blockchain model to Bitcoin,
we can refine some of the swappability results presented before.
In particular, in Bitcoin we can easily construct safe approximations
of the observables read/written by a transaction, by just considering
their inputs and outputs (Lemma
10
).
Further, while strong swappability is stricter than swappability
(Example
3.1
),
strong and parameterized strong swappability coincide in Bitcoin
(Theorem
13
).

The following lemma provides the least safe approximations
of the observables read and written by consistent transactions.
For inconsistent transactions, these approximations are just
the empty set (Lemma
11
).
Intuitively, the observables written by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
can be approximated as

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
, because
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
spends all the
transaction outputs in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
,
and creates the transaction outputs in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
.
Instead, the read observables can be approximated as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
,
since by Definition
2.1
,
executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
from two states which agree on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}

leads to two states which only differ on the observables
for which they differed before.

Lemma 10
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
be a consistent Bitcoin transaction, and let:

W\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}\qquad\qquad R\;=\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}

Then,
W
(resp.
R
) is the least safe approximation
of written (resp. read) observables.

Proof 3.3
.

We first show that
W
and
R
are safe approximations
of written / read observables,
and then that they are the least ones.

•

W
is a safe approximation of written observables.

Let
Q
be such that
Q\cap({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out})=\emptyset
.
For all blockchain states
\sigma
,
we have that
\mbox{$\llbracket{\varepsilon}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma
, and:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma^{\prime}\qquad\text{where }U_{\sigma^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}(U_{\sigma}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in})\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}&\text{if $\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}$}\\
U_{\sigma}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Since
Q
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
are disjoint,
we have that
\sigma\sim_{Q}\sigma^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
.
By Definition
3
, it follows that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.

•

R
is a safe approximation of read observables.

Assume that
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\sim_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is valid in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
iff it is valid in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
.
Let
Q
be such that
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
,
let
\sigma_{0}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}
and
\sigma_{1}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket$}
.
For
i\in\{0,1\}
, we have that:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma_{i}}$}=\sigma_{i}^{\prime}\qquad\text{where }U_{\sigma_{i}^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}(U_{\sigma_{i}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in})\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}&\text{if $\sigma_{i}\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}$}\\
U_{\sigma_{i}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Since
\sigma_{0}\sim_{Q}\sigma_{1}
, it follows that

\sigma_{0}^{\prime}\sim_{Q}\sigma_{1}^{\prime}
,
and therefore
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
By Definition
3
, it follows that

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.

•

W
is the least safe approximation of written observables.

By contradiction,
let
W^{\prime}\subsetneq W

be such that
{W^{\prime}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,
and let
R^{\prime}\subsetneq R

be such that
{R^{\prime}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.
Let
{p}\in W\setminus W^{\prime}
.
Since
\{{p}\}\cap W^{\prime}=\emptyset
and

{W^{\prime}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{\{{p}\}}\varepsilon
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is consistent, there exists
\sigma
such that

\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Then,

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma^{\prime}
, where

U_{\sigma^{\prime}}=(U_{\sigma}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in})\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
.
Since
{p}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
,
it follows that
\sigma^{\prime}{p}\neq\sigma{p}
, and so

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\not\sim_{\{{p}\}}\varepsilon
— contradiction.
Therefore,
W
is the least approximation of the observables
written by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

•

R
is the least safe approximation of read observables.

Let
Q={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is consistent,
there exists a reachable
\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\sigma^{\prime}\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Since
\sigma^{\prime}
is reachable, there exists
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
such that

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}=\sigma^{\prime}
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
, where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
spends
R\setminus R^{\prime}
.
Then,
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{R^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
.
Since
{R^{\prime}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
, it must be

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Since in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
some of the inputs needed by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
have been spent,
we have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is not valid in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
, and so

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket$}
.
On the other hand, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is valid in
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
, then

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket$}=\sigma^{\prime\prime}
, where

U_{\sigma^{\prime\prime}}=(U_{\sigma^{\prime}}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in})\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
.
Since
Q={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
belongs to
\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket

but not to
\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket
,
it follows that
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\not\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
— contradiction.
Therefore,
R
is the least approximation of the observables
read by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

Lemma 11
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is inconsistent if and only if

{\emptyset}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
and
{\emptyset}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.

Proof 3.4
.

For the “only if” part, assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is inconsistent.
For
{}\models^{w}{}
, for all
\sigma
we have that

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma=\mbox{$\llbracket{\varepsilon}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}
.
Therefore, for all
Q
e have
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}\varepsilon
,
from which it follow that
{\emptyset}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.
For all
{}\models^{r}{}
, for all
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
and
Q
we have that
if
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
then
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Therefore,
{\emptyset}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.
For the “if” part, assume that
{\emptyset}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.
Then, for all
Q
it must be
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}\varepsilon
,
i.e.
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim\varepsilon
.
By definition of
\sim
this implies that, for all
\sigma
,

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{\varepsilon}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma
.
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is not valid in any blockchain state
\sigma
,
and so
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is inconsistent.

By exploiting the results above, we can provide an alternative
sufficient condition for (strong) swappability.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is valid in some state
where it is also possible to append another transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}

before
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
(i.e.,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
is valid in that state),
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
are strongly swappable.
This is a peculiar property of UTXO-based blockchains like Bitcoin:
in Example
3.2
we show that this is not
the case for Ethereum.

Lemma 12
.

In Bitcoin,
if there exists
\sigma
such that

\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
.

Proof 3.5
.

Let
\sigma
be such that
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.
Then, by condition (
1
)
of Definition
2.1
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}\;\;=\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}\;\;=\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\;\;=\;\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}\cap{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}\;\;=\;\;\emptyset

(1)

By Lemma
10
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}

are safe approximations of written/read observables.
By (
1
), these approximations satisfy
the condition of Definition
3
,
and so
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
.

The following example shows that the converse of
Theorem
5
does not hold in Bitcoin, i.e. there exist transactions which are swappable but not strongly swappable.

{exa}

[Swappable transactions, but not strongly]

Consider the transactions in Figure
2
,
where the scripts and currency values are immaterial
(we just assume that condition (
2
)
of Definition
2.1
is satisfied for each
matching input/output pair).
We show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
are swappable.
Let
\sigma
be a blockchain state.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
is not valid in
\sigma
,
then
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}

holds trivially, since also
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
is not valid.
Otherwise, if
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
:

\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma^{\prime}}$}

where
U_{\sigma^{\prime}}=(U_{\sigma}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in})\cup\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},1),\,({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},2)\}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}

since
\sigma^{\prime}\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\rhd}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\rhd}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\rhd}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\rhd}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
, as
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}},1)\not\in U_{\sigma^{\prime}}

\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}

since
\sigma\mathchoice{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\displaystyle\not$\hss}{\rhd}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.8889pt\kern-5.27776pt$\textstyle\not$\hss}{\rhd}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 3.125pt\kern-4.45831pt$\scriptstyle\not$\hss}{\rhd}}}{\mathrel{\hbox to0.0pt{\kern 2.70836pt\kern-3.95834pt$\scriptscriptstyle\not$\hss}{\rhd}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
, as
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},1)\not\in U_{\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{\prime}

where
U_{\sigma^{\prime}}=(U_{\sigma}\setminus{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in})\cup\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},1),\,({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},2)\}

Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
are swappable.
We now show that they are not
strongly
swappable.
Assume that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
is consistent.
Then, by Lemma
10
it follows that

W_{1}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
is the least safe approximation
of the observables written by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
.
Let
\sigma
be such that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
is valid in
\sigma
.
Then,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
is valid in
\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}
,
and so by Lemma
10
is also follows that

W_{3}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}

is the least safe approximation of the observables written by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
.
Since
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},1)\in W_{1}\cap W_{3}
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
are not strongly swappable.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}({1}):{\cdots}

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}({1}):{\cdots}

{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}({2}):{\cdots}

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}

in
(1):
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},2)

out
(1):
\cdots

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}

in
(1):
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},1)

in
(2):
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}},1)

out
(1):
\cdots

Figure 2
.
Transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
are swappable but not strongly swappable.

Finally, we prove that
strong and parameterized strong swappability coincide in Bitcoin.

Theorem 13
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
be consistent Bitcoin transactions.
If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
,
using the static analyses
\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
and

\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}
.

Proof 3.6
.

Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
, then there exist safe approximations

{W}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,

{W^{\prime}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}
,

{R}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
, and

{R^{\prime}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}

such that

\big(R\cup W\big)\cap W^{\prime}=\emptyset=\big(R^{\prime}\cup W^{\prime}\big)\cap W
.
Since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
are consistent,
then by Lemma
10

the static analyses
\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf out}
and

\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}.{\color[rgb]{0.6,0.25,0.6}\sf in}

give their
least
safe approximation of written/read observables.
Then,
\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\subseteq W
,
\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})\subseteq W^{\prime}
,

\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})\subseteq R
, and
\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}})\subseteq R^{\prime}
.
Then, the disjointess condition required by Definition
3

holds for the static analyses, and so
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
.

3.2.
Swapping transactions in Ethereum

We now illustrate our notions of swappability in Ethereum
through a series of examples.
We postpone to
Section

5
a discussion on how to
approximate the observables read/written by Ethereum contracts,
so to compute the parameterized strong swappability relation.

{exa}

[Swappability]
Recall the contract
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}
and the blockchain

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}

from Example
2.2
.
By Definition
3
, we have that:

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\rightleftarrows{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
(see
Figure

3
, top left).
Indeed, regardless of whether
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
is appended to the blockchain
before or after
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
, after their execution we obtain the same state:
\sigma^{\prime}\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}
, when
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\geq 1
, and
\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}
otherwise.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\mbox{$\,\not\rightleftarrows\,$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
(see
Figure

3
, top right).
Let
\sigma
be such that
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x\neq 0
and
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}=1
.
If we append
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
before
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
we obtain the state
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1
and in this state
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
is idempotent.
Instead, the result of executing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
before
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
is the state
\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:1
and in this state
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
is idempotent.
Therefore,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\mbox{$\,\not\rightleftarrows\,$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\mbox{$\,\not\rightleftarrows\,$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
(see
Figure

3
, bottom).
Depending on how we append
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
we obtain two different states.
Let
\sigma
be such that
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}=0
and
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\geq 1
.
If we append
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
before
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
we obtain the state
\sigma^{\prime}\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}
.
Instead, if we append
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
and then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
we obtain the state
\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\geq 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}<1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\geq 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}<1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}

(a)

Proof of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\rightleftarrows{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}\neq 0\\
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}=1\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime\prime}=\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}

(b)

Proof of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\mbox{$\,\not\rightleftarrows\,$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma^{\prime}\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\scriptsize\begin{array}[]{l}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}=0\\
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\geq 1\end{array}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}
/\penalty
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}

(c)

Proof of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\mbox{$\,\not\rightleftarrows\,$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
.

Figure 3
.
Proofs for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\rightleftarrows{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\mbox{$\,\not\rightleftarrows\,$}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
. A transition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
from
\sigma
can be taken only if the guard below the arrow is satisfied in
\sigma
.

{exa}

[Strong swappability]
Let
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}
be the contract of Example
2.2
, and let

{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{3}}}({})\{{{\tt{skip}}}\}
be a function of a contract
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{D}}}
.
Then, consider the following transactions:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{D}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{3}}}({})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{F}}}})

where
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
,
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{F}}}
are account addresses.
Intuitively,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
because they are operating on observables of different addresses.
Formally, consider the following safe approximations of the written/read observables of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
:

\displaystyle{W_{3}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{D}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}}

\displaystyle{R_{3}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}}

\displaystyle{W_{4}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{F}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}}

\displaystyle{R_{4}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}}

Since
(W_{3}\cup R_{3})\cap W_{4}=\emptyset=(W_{4}\cup R_{4})\cap W_{3}
, the two transactions are strongly swappable.

Now, consider the following transaction that calls the function
{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}
with the address
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}}({{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}})

This transaction transfers
1
currency unit from
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
to
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
.
Intuitively, since
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}
touches
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}
,
then it should not be swappable with
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}
.
Formally, consider the following safe approximations
W_{5}
and
R_{5}
:

\displaystyle{W_{5}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}},{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\}}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}}\qquad{R_{5}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}}

Since
W_{3}\cap W_{5}\neq\emptyset\neq W_{4}\cap W_{5}
,
then
\neg({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{3}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}})
and

\neg({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{4}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}})
.

The following example shows that the converse of
Theorem
5
does not hold,
i.e. there may exist transactions that are swappable but not strongly swappable.
This is because of static analyses could produce false negatives.

{exa}

[Swappable transactions, but not strongly]

Consider the following functions of a contract
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}
,
and the following transactions sent by users
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
:

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{1}}}({})\{{{\tt{if}}\,{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}\;\textup{{\char 38\relax\char 38\relax}}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{1}}}}=0}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{1}}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}\,{\tt{else}}\,{{\tt{throw}}}}\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{1}}}({})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}}({})\{{{\tt{if}}\,{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{sender}}}={\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}\;\textup{{\char 38\relax\char 38\relax}}\;{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{2}}}}=0}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{2}}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}\,{\tt{else}}\,{{\tt{throw}}}}\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}}({})

We have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
are swappable.
To see why, consider the following two cases:

(1)

a state
\sigma
where
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}>1
,

\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}>1
,
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}=n
,

\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{1}}}=0
and
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{2}}}=0
.
In
\sigma
it holds that:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{1}}}}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{2}}}}},\nicefrac{{n+2}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}}}\}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}

(2)

a state
\sigma
such that
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}<1
,
or
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}<1
, or
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{1}}}\neq 0
,
or
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{2}}}\neq 0
.
Since it is not possible that the guards of
{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{2}}

are both true, one of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}

or
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
raises an exception, leaving the state unaffected.
Then, also in this case we have that

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}

However,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
are
not
strongly swappable.
Intuitively, this is because there exist reachable states
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
such that
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{1}}}=0=\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{2}}}
.
Formally, consider the following sets

\displaystyle W_{1}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}.{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}}.{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}}.{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{1}}}}\}

\displaystyle W_{2}=\{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}.{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}}.{{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{1}}}.{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k_{2}}}}\}

which are the least safe over-approximations of the written observables
by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}

and by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
,
respectively.
This means that every safe approximation of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
must include the observables of
W_{1}
, and similarly for the set
W_{2}
.
Since
W_{1}\cap W_{2}\neq\emptyset
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
does not hold.

The following example shows that Lemma
12
,
which is specific to Bitcoin and UTXO-based blockchains,
does not hold on Ethereum.

{exa}

Recall the functions
{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{0}}}({})\{{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}\}

and
{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{1}}}({})\{{{\tt{if}}\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}=0}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}}.\texttt{transfer}({1}){}{}}}\}

from Example
2.2
,
and consider the following transactions:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{1}}}({})\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{1}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{0}}}({})

Let
\sigma
be a state such that
\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x=0

and
\sigma\,{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}.{\tt{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{balance}}}\geq 2
.
We have that
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
, and so

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:1+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:1
.
Further,
\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and

\sigma\rhd{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}
.
Then:

\sigma_{5,1}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:2+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}:1\qquad\sigma_{1,5}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}-{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}:2+{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}:1

Hence,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{5}}
are not swappable, because

\sigma_{1,5}
and
\sigma_{5,1}
differ in the balances of
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{B}}}
.

4.
True concurrency for blockchains

Given a swappability relation
\mathcal{R}
,
we transform a sequence of transactions
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
into an

occurrence net

{\sf N}_{\mathcal{R}}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
,
which describes the partial order induced by
\mathcal{R}
.
Our main result is that any concurrent execution of the transactions in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}

which respects this partial order is equivalent
to the serial execution of
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
(Theorem
16
).

Occurrence nets

We start by recapping the notion of Petri net
[
Rei85
]
.
A
Petri net
is a tuple

\sf{N}=({\sf P},{\sf Tr},{\sf{F}},{\sf{m}_{0}})
,
where
{\sf P}
is a set of
places
,

{\sf Tr}
is a set of
transitions

(with
{\sf P}\cap{\sf Tr}=\emptyset
),
and

{\sf{F}}:({\sf P}\times{\sf Tr})\cup({\sf Tr}\times{\sf P})\rightarrow\mathbb{N}

is a
weight function
.
The state of a net is a
marking
, i.e. a multiset

{\sf{m}}:{\sf P}\rightarrow\mathbb{N}
defining how many
tokens

are contained in each place;
we denote with
{\sf{m}_{0}}
the initial marking.
The behaviour of a Petri net is specified as
a transition relation between markings:
intuitively, a transition
\mathsf{t}
is enabled at
{\sf{m}}

when each place
{\sf{p}}

has at least
{\sf{F}}({\sf{p}},\mathsf{t})
tokens in
{\sf{m}}
.
When an enabled transition
\mathsf{t}
is fired, it consumes

{\sf{F}}({\sf{p}},\mathsf{t})
tokens from each
{\sf{p}}
,
and produces
{\sf{F}}(\mathsf{t},{\sf{p^{\prime}}})
tokens in each
{\sf{p^{\prime}}}
.
Formally, given
x\in{\sf P}\cup{\sf Tr}
,
we define the
preset

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{x}
and the
postset

{x}{{}^{\bullet}}

as multisets:

{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{x}(y)={\sf{F}}(y,x)
, and

{x}{{}^{\bullet}}(y)={\sf{F}}(x,y)
.
A transition
\mathsf{t}
is
enabled
at
{\sf{m}}

when
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mathsf{t}}\subseteq{\sf{m}}
.
The transition relation between markings is defined as

{\sf{m}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{t}}{\sf{m}}-{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{\mathsf{t}}+{\mathsf{t}}{{}^{\bullet}}
,
where
\mathsf{t}
is enabled.
We say that
\mathsf{t}_{1}\cdots\mathsf{t}_{n}
is a

firing sequence from
{\sf{m}}
to
{\sf{m}^{\prime}}
when

{\sf{m}}\xrightarrow{\mathsf{t}_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{\mathsf{t}_{n}}{\sf{m}^{\prime}}
,
and in this case we say that

{\sf{m}^{\prime}}
is
reachable from

{\sf{m}}
.
We say that
{\sf{m}^{\prime}}
is
reachable
when it is reachable from
{\sf{m}_{0}}
.

An
occurrence net

[
BD87
]
is a Petri net such that:

1

|{{\sf{p}}}{{}^{\bullet}}|\leq 1
for all
{\sf{p}}
;

2

|{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{{\sf{p}}}|=1
if
{\sf{p}}\not\in{\sf{m}_{0}}
, and

|{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{{\sf{p}}}|=0
if
{\sf{p}}\in{\sf{m}_{0}}
;

3

{\sf{F}}
is a relation, i.e.
{\sf{F}}(x,y)\leq 1
for all
x,y
;

4

{\sf{F}}^{*}
is a acyclic, i.e.
\forall x,y\in{\sf P}\cup{\sf Tr}:(x,y)\in{\sf{F}}^{*}\land(y,x)\in{\sf{F}}^{*}\implies x=y

(where
{\sf{F}}^{*}
is the reflexive and transitive closure of
{\sf{F}}
).

From blockchains to occurrence nets

We describe in Figure
4
how to transform
a blockchain
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}}

into a Petri net
{\sf N}_{\mathcal{R}}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
,
where
\mathcal{R}
is an arbitrary relation between transactions.
Although any relation
\mathcal{R}
ensures that
{\sf N}_{\mathcal{R}}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})

is an occurrence net (Lemma
14
),
our main results hold when
\mathcal{R}
is a strong swappability relation.
The transformation works as follows:
the
i
-th transaction in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
is rendered as a transition
({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}},i)
in
{\sf N}_{\mathcal{R}}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
, and
transactions related by
\mathcal{R}
are transformed into concurrent transitions.
Technically, this concurrency is specified as
a relation
<
between transitions, such that

({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}},i)<({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{j}},j)
whenever
i<j
,
but
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{j}}
are not related by
\mathcal{R}
.
The places, the weight function, and the initial marking of
{\sf N}_{\mathcal{R}}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})

are chosen to ensure that the firing ot transitions respects the relation
<
.

\displaystyle{\sf Tr}=\left\{{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}},i)}\,\middle|\,{1\leq i\leq n}\right\}\qquad\qquad{\sf P}\;=\left\{{(*,\mathsf{t})}\,\middle|\,{\mathsf{t}\in{\sf Tr}}\right\}\cup\left\{{(\mathsf{t},*)}\,\middle|\,{\mathsf{t}\in{\sf Tr}}\right\}\cup\left\{{(\mathsf{t},{{\sf{t}}^{\prime}})}\,\middle|\,{\mathsf{t}<{{\sf{t}}^{\prime}}}\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 188.0pt\text{where }({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},i)<({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}},j)\triangleq(i<j)\,\land\,\neg({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\,\mathcal{R}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}})

\displaystyle{\sf{F}}(x,y)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $y=\mathsf{t}$ and \big($x=(*,\mathsf{t})$ or $x=({{\sf{t}}^{\prime}},\mathsf{t})$\big)}\\
1&\text{if $x=\mathsf{t}$ and \big($y=(\mathsf{t},*)$ or $y=(\mathsf{t},{{\sf{t}}^{\prime}})$\big)}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\hskip 40.0pt{\sf{m}_{0}}({\sf{p}})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if ${\sf{p}}=(*,\mathsf{t})$}\\
0&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

Figure 4
.
Construction of a Petri net from a blockchain
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}}
.

Lemma 14
.

{\sf N}_{\mathcal{R}}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
is an occurrence net, for all
\mathcal{R}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
.

Step firing sequences

Theorem
16
establishes a correspondence between
concurrent and serial execution of transactions.
Since the semantics of serial executions is given in terms of blockchain states
\sigma
,
to formalise this correspondence we use the same semantics domain
also for concurrent executions.
This is obtained in two steps.
First, we define concurrent executions of
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}

as the
step firing sequences
(i.e. finite sequences of
sets
of transitions)
of
{\sf N}_{\#}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
.
Then, we give a semantics to step firing sequences, in terms of blockchain states.

We denote finite sets of transitions, called
steps
,
as
{\sf U},{\sf U^{\prime}},\ldots
.
Their preset and postset are defined as

\textstyle{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{{\sf U}}=\sum_{{\sf{p}}\in{\sf U}}{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{{\sf{p}}}

and

\textstyle{{\sf U}}{{}^{\bullet}}=\sum_{{\sf{p}}\in{\sf U}}{{\sf{p}}}{{}^{\bullet}}
,
respectively.
We say that
{\sf U}
is
enabled at
{\sf{m}}

when
{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{{\sf U}}\leq{\sf{m}}
, and in this case
firing
{\sf U}
results in the move

{\sf{m}}\xrightarrow{{\sf U}}{\sf{m}}-{\rm Phys.~Rev.~E}{{\sf U}}+{{\sf U}}{{}^{\bullet}}
.
Let
\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}={\sf U}_{1}\cdots{\sf U}_{n}
be a finite sequence of steps.
We say that
\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}
is a

step firing sequence from
{\sf{m}}
to
{\sf{m}^{\prime}}

if
{\sf{m}}\xrightarrow{{\sf U}_{1}}\cdots\xrightarrow{{\sf U}_{n}}{\sf{m}^{\prime}}
,
and in this case we write

{\sf{m}}\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}}{\sf{m}^{\prime}}
.

Concurrent execution of transactions

To execute transactions in parallel,
the idea is to execute them in
isolation
,
and then merge their changes, whenever they are disjoint.
The state updates
\pi
resulting from the execution of a transaction
are formalised as in Section
2
.

An
update collector
is a function
\Pi
that,
given a state
\sigma
and a transaction
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
,
gives a state update
\pi=\Pi(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})
which maps (at least)
the updated observables to their new values.
In practice, update collectors can be obtained by instrumenting
the run-time environment of blockchains,
to record the state updates resulting from the execution of transactions.
We formalise update collectors in Definition
4

by abstracting from the implementation details of such an instrumentation:

{defi}

[Update collector]

We say that a function
\Pi
is an
update collector
when

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma(\Pi(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}))
,
for all
\sigma
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
.

There exists a natural ordering of update collectors,
which extends the ordering between state updates
(i.e., set inclusion, when interpreting them as sets of substitutions):
namely,
\Pi\sqsubseteq\Pi^{\prime}
holds when

\forall\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}:\Pi(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})\subseteq\Pi^{\prime}(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})
.
The following lemma characterizes the least
update collector w.r.t. this ordering.

Lemma 15

(Least update collector)
.

Let
\Pi^{\star}(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}-\sigma
,
where we define
\sigma^{\prime}-\sigma
as

\bigcup_{{\sigma^{\prime}{p}\neq\sigma{p}}}\{\nicefrac{{\sigma^{\prime}{p}}}{{{p}}}\}
.
Then,
\Pi^{\star}
is the least update collector.

The merge of two state updates is the union of the corresponding substitutions;
to avoid collisions, we make the merge undefined
when the domains of the two updates overlap.

{defi}

[Merge of state updates]

Let
\pi_{0}
,
\pi_{1}
be state updates.
When
\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{0}}\cap\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{1}}=\emptyset
,
we define
\pi_{0}\oplus\pi_{1}
as follows:

(\pi_{0}\oplus\pi_{1}){p}=\begin{cases}\pi_{0}{p}&\text{if ${p}\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{0}}$}\\
\pi_{1}{p}&\text{if ${p}\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{1}}$}\\
\bot&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

The merge operator enjoys the commutative monoidal laws, and can therefore be
extended to (finite) sets of state updates.

We now associate step firing sequences with state updates.
The semantics of a step
{\sf U}=\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},1),\ldots,({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}},n)\}

in
\sigma
is obtained by applying to
\sigma

the merge of the updates
\Pi(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{i}})
,
for all
i\in 1..n
— whenever the merge is defined.
The semantics of a step firing sequence is then obtained by
folding the semantics of its steps.

{defi}

[Semantics of step firing sequences]

We define the semantics of step firing sequences,
given
\Pi
and
\sigma
, as:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\sigma}$}\;=\;\sigma\hskip 30.0pt\mbox{$\llbracket{{\sf U}\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\sigma}$}\;=\;\mbox{$\llbracket{\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\sigma^{\prime}}$}\quad\text{where }\sigma^{\prime}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\sf U}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\sigma}$}=\sigma\bigoplus_{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}},i)\in{\sf U}}\Pi(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})

Concurrent execution of blockchains

Theorem
16
below relates serial executions of transactions
to concurrent ones (which are rendered as step firing sequences).
Item
1
establishes a confluence property:
if two step firing sequences lead to the same marking,
then they also lead to the same blockchain state.
Item
2
ensures that the blockchain,
interpreted as a sequence of transitions, is a step firing sequence,
and it is
maximal

(i.e., there is a bijection between the transactions in the blockchain
and the transitions of the corresponding net).
Finally, item
3

ensures that executing maximal step firing sequences
is equivalent to executing serially the entire blockchain.

Theorem 16
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\cdots{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}}
.
Then, in
{\sf N}_{\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
:

(1)

if
{\sf{m}_{0}}\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}}{\sf{m}}
and

{\sf{m}_{0}}\xrightarrow{\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime}}}}{\sf{m}}
,
then

\mbox{$\llbracket{\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}
,
for all reachable
\sigma
;

(2)

\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},1)\}\cdots\{({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}},n)\}

is a maximal step firing sequence;

(3)

for all maximal step firing sequences
\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}
,
for all reachable
\sigma
,

\mbox{$\llbracket{\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}
.

(*,\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}})
(*,\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}})
(*,\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}})
(\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},*)
(\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}},*)
(\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}},*)
(\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}})
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}

Figure 5
.
Occurrence net for Example
4
.

{exa}

[Occurrence Net construction in Ethereum]

Consider the following Ethereum transactions and functions of a contract
{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{0}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}({})\,\{{\tt{if}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}}=0}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{y}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}\,{\tt{else}}\,{{\tt{throw}}}\}
