\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{0}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}({})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}({})\,\{{\tt{if}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{y}}}=0}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}\,{\tt{else}}\,{{\tt{throw}}}\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}={{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{A}}}}\xrightarrow{0}{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}}:{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}({})

\displaystyle{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}({})\,\{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{z}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}\}

Let the following sets be safe approximations of the corresponding transactions

P_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}^{w}=P_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}^{r}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{y}}\},P_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}^{r}=P_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}^{w}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}\},P_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}^{w}=\{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{z}}\},P_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}^{r}=\emptyset

where the subscript denotes the transaction and the superscript denotes if the set approximates the read or written keys, e.g.,
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}^{w}
safely approximates the keys written by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}
, whereas
P_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}^{r}
safely approximates the keys read by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}
.

By Definition
3
we have that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}
, but
\neg({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}\#{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}})
.
By instantiating the construction of Figure
4
using the relation
\#
, we obtain the occurrence net
{\sf N}_{\#}({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}})
of Figure
5
, where
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}},1)
,

\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}},2)
, and

\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}},3)
.
From this occurrence net is easy to see that transition
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}
can only be fired after
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}
,
while
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}
can be fired independently from

\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}
and
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}
.
This is coherent with the fact that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}
is swappable with
both
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}
, while
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}

are not swappable.

Recall that
\Pi^{\star}
is the least update collector, i.e. a function that given a state
\sigma
returns the minimal update
\pi
mapping qualified keys to their new values.
To run in parallel the transactions, we execute them in isolation and then we merge their effect, by merging their state updates.
For example, given a state
\sigma
such that

\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x=\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y=0
the minimal updates for

\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}
, and
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}
are:

\Pi^{\star}(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}})=\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}}\}\qquad\Pi^{\star}(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}})=\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}\qquad\Pi^{\star}(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}})=\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.z}}\}

By Definition
4
the parallel execution of
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}
, and
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}
in
\sigma
results in the following states

\displaystyle\mbox{$\llbracket{\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}\}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}=\sigma(\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}}\}\oplus\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.z}}\})=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.z}}\}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\llbracket{\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}\}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}=\sigma(\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}\oplus\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.z}}\})=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.z}}\}

\displaystyle\mbox{$\llbracket{\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}\}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}=(\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}}\}\oplus\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\})=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}

Note that, for all
\sigma
the serial execution of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}

(in both orders) is equal to their concurrent execution
(similarly for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}
):

\begin{array}[]{c}\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}\;=\;\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}\;=\;\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.z}}\}\;=\;\mbox{$\llbracket{\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}\}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}\\
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}\;=\;\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}\;=\;\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.z}}\}\;=\;\mbox{$\llbracket{\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}\}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}\end{array}

Instead, for all
\sigma
such that
\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}=\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{y}}=0
the concurrent executions of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}
may
differ from serial ones:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}}\}\qquad\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}\qquad\mbox{$\llbracket{\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}\}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}=\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}},\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.x}}\}

This is due the fact that
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}
and
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}
are
not
concurrent in the occurrence net of Figure
5
.

Now let
\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}=\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}\}\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}\}
be a maximal step firing sequence of
{\sf N}_{\#}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
.
Since
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}
and
\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}
are concurrent by item
3
of Theorem
16
we can conclude that the semantics of
\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}
in the state
\sigma
is equivalent to the serial one of
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}}
:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\sigma}$}\;=\;\sigma\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.y}}\}\{\nicefrac{{1}}{{{\color[rgb]{0.13,0.55,0.13}{\mathcal{C}}}.z}}\}\;=\;\mbox{$\llbracket{\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}

It is worth noticing that any other maximal step firing sequence of
{\sf N}_{\#}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
results in the same state.
For example, consider
\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime}}}=\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}}\}\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}\}
,
where the places
(\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},*)
,
(\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}},*)
and
(\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}},*)

contain one token each, while the other places have no tokens.
Since
\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}
and
\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime}}}
lead to the same marking,
by item
1
of
16

we conclude that

\mbox{$\llbracket{\boldsymbol{{\sf U}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi^{\star}}_{\sigma}$}

Now, consider
\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime\prime}}}=\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{h}}}}\}\{\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}},\mathsf{t}_{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{g}}}}\}
.
Although
\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime\prime}}}
is maximal, it is not a step firing sequence,
since the second step is not enabled, therefore, no items of
16
apply to
\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime\prime}}}
.
This is coherent with the fact that
\boldsymbol{{\sf U^{\prime\prime}}}
does not represent any sequential execution of
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
.

5.
Experimental validation

In this
section
we discuss
how to exploit our theoretical results in practice
to improve the performance of blockchain nodes.
We start by sketching the algorithm used by miners and validators
to construct blocks.
Miners should perform the following steps:

(1)

gather from the network a set of transactions,
and put them in an arbitrary linear order
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
, which is the mined block;

(2)

compute the relation
\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}
on
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
, using a
static analysis of read/written observables;

(3)

construct the occurrence net
{\sf N}_{\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}}({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}})
;

(4)

execute the transactions in
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
concurrently
according to the occurrence net,
exploiting the available parallelism.

The protocol followed by validators is almost identical to that of miners:
the main difference is that step
1
is skipped,
and at step
2
,
the relation
\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}
is computed starting from the block
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}

to be validated.
Note that the static analysis used by a validator could be different
from the analysis used by the node which mined
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
, and therefore
the occurrence net could be different from that used by the miner.
However, this is not a problem:
from item
3
of
16
it follows
that executing
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
on
any occurrence nets built on any static analysis of read/written variables
leads to the same state.
In this way, blocks do not need to carry the occurrence net as metadata:
this makes our approach is compatible with any blockchain platform,
without requiring a soft-fork.

For the case of Bitcoin, we argue that implementing this algorithm is
straightforward:
indeed, Lemma
10

allows to compute the strong swappability relation
directly from the transactions inputs and outputs.
For Ethereum the problem is more complex, since the algorithm relies
on a static analysis of the observables read/written by transactions.
Therefore, in the rest of this
section
we
evaluate the feasibility of our approach on Ethereum.
To this purpose,
we implement a prototype analyser of Ethereum bytecode,
and we evaluate its precision on a relevant contract.
We then compare the time of sequential executions of blocks
against their parallel executions
(which includes the time for the static analysis).
Despite the limitations of the static analysis tool
(that we discuss at the end of the section),
we find that our technique improves the execution time in our experiment.

Analysing Ethereum bytecode

In general, precise static analyses at the level of the
Ethereum bytecode are difficult to achieve,
since the language has features like dynamic dispatching and pointer aliasing
which are notoriously a source of imprecision for static analysis.
As far as we know, none of the analysis tools for Ethereum contracts
exports an over-approximation of read/written keys
which is usable to the purpose of this paper.
The only tool we know of that outputs such an over-approximation
is ES-ETH
[
Mar19
]
, but it has several limitations
which make its output too coarse to be usable in practice.
So, to perform an empirical validation of our approach
we develop a new prototypical tool
[
Tos20a
]
.
Our tool takes as input the EVM bytecode of a contract
and a sequence of transactions,
and gives as output the occurrence net,
using the construction in
Section

4
.
The tool implements as a standalone library
[
Tos20b
]

a static analysis that over-approximates the read and written keys
for each function of a given smart contract.

Before presenting the design underlying our static analyzer,
we briefly recall the EVM memory model and how the bytecode generated by Solidity compiler is organized (see
[
Eth21
,
Woo14
]

for further details).
The execution of a smart contract involves three kinds of memory:

1

the
world state
,
i.e. a mapping from addresses to account information
(e.g., balances, functions, etc.);

2

the
contract storage
, mapping keys to values;

3

the
working memory
, i.e. a stack which stores
function parameters, local variables and temporary values
created during the function execution.

The EVM machine features instructions to load and store values
from these memories, e.g., SSTORE and SLOAD operate
on the world state.

The Solidity compiler splits the generated bytecode in two sections:
the
constructor code
and the
runtime code
.
The constructor code is executed upon contract creation,
and typically returns the runtime code to be deployed on the blockchain.
The runtime code is executed upon a function call.
This code first initializes the contract storage and the stack,
and then transfers the control to the body of the function called
in the transaction.

Our static analysis symbolically executes both the constructor and runtime
code.
Since EVM bytecode has no explicit notion of function declaration,
we analyze the constructor code and the first part of the runtime code
to detect which functions are declared in the contract and
where their code is located.
Once we identify the functions, we analyze their code separately.
For each function we compute three sets:
the sets of keys that are read/written by the function,
and the set of calls made to external contracts.
To construct these sets we exploit a symbolic semantics
of EVM instructions, that operates on abstract versions of the stack
and memory storage.
Intuitively, the analysis of each instruction
results in an abstract value,
specifying the operation performed and the affected keys.

(*,\mathsf{t}_{n})
\mathsf{t}_{n}
(\mathsf{t}_{n},*)
\mathsf{t}_{j_{0}}

Experiments

We experimentally validate our approach
by estimating the potential speed up achieved by running transactions in parallel.
To this purpose we consider a contract
which implements a two-players lottery
(see Listing
in the Appendix for its Solidity code).
Intuitively, a user who wants
to participate in the lottery performs the following steps:

(1)

join the game by sending a certain amount of cryptocurrency, representing the bid;

(2)

commit to a secret string by sending its hash,
which is stored on the contract state;

(3)

once both players have completed the commit phase,
they can reveal their secrets, independently from each other;

(4)

once both players have revealed, anyone can call the win function
to transfer the bets to the winner, who is determined according to the parity of the length of players’ secrets.

Once the contract has been initialized (with transaction
\mathsf{t}_{n}
),
a complete execution of the lottery then requires 7 transactions:

\mathsf{t}_{j_{0}}
,
\mathsf{t}_{c_{0}}
,
\mathsf{t}_{r_{0}}
,
representing the join, commit and reveal
of the first player,

\mathsf{t}_{j_{1}}
,
\mathsf{t}_{c_{1}}
,
\mathsf{t}_{r_{1}}
for the second player,
and
\mathsf{t}_{w}
for invoking the win function.

Figure

6
displays the occurrence net computed by our tool
from a single complete execution of the lottery.
The occurrence net shows that
players can join, commit and reveal
independently from each other.
However, the commit transactions can be fired
only after both join have been fired,
while the reveal transactions can be fired only after both commit.
Further, the win transaction can be fired only after both players
have revealed their secrets.

To estimate the possible speed up obtained by running the transactions in parallel, we play the whole lottery 10 times,
generating a total amount of 70 transactions (besides the contract creation).
We first run these transactions sequentially, and measure the execution time of each transaction.
Then, we use our tool to find a parallel schedule,
and compute the time spent if the transactions were run in parallel.

We carry out our experiments on a laptop machine with Intel Core i5-3320M CPU @ 2.60GHz and 4Gb of RAM.
4
4

4

Our scripts and data are available online at
https://github.com/lillo/lmcs-analysis-validation

We use geth
5
5

5

https://geth.ethereum.org/

to setup a development chain,
and Truffle
6
6

6

https://www.trufflesuite.com/

to deploy a local instance of the Lottery contract on this chain.

We first compute the sequential execution time by summing up the time spent for running each transaction,
as reported by the logs of geth.
The first column of
Table

1
displays the time of sequential execution, averaged over 10 measurements.

Then, we analyze the sequence of transactions using our tool,
obtaining the occurrence net.
The second column of
Table

1
displays the average time spent
by the tool to analyze the transactions and to build the occurrence net
(again, the measurements are repeated for 10 times).
From the occurrence net, we estimate the average time required
by the most expensive parallel schedule.
This schedule is computed as the longest and most expensive path (in terms of time) of the occurrence net.
The time required to execute this schedule is in the third column of
Table

1
.
Note that estimating the cost of the parallel execution in this way
implies that we are assuming to have a sufficient number of threads
to execute the transactions
(in the Lottery experiment, two threads are enough),
and that once a transaction is assigned to a thread it is executed with
no latency or queuing time.
Finally, the fourth column of
Table

1
displays
the total time required to analyze the transactions
and to run them in parallel.

Although the experiment is carried with simplifying assumptions
and on a single contract,
the results of
Table

1
are a first empirical evidence
of the practical applicability of our approach,
and that parallelizing the execution of transaction
may lead to performance improvements in Ethereum nodes.
We discuss below some current limitations and possible improvements
of our experimental validation.

Limitations and possible improvements

The current version of our static analysis tool of Ethereum bytecode
has been developed under some simplifying assumptions.
First, the tool can only analyse contracts whose bytecode respects
the following conditions,
which are always satisfied for bytecode obtained by the Solidity compiler:

1

the constructor code always returns the runtime code;

2

the runtime code does not access the world state in response to a call with an
invalid function signature;

3

when a transaction calls a valid function,
the runtime code always transfers the control to the body of the function.

While the tool could be adapted to updates of the Solidy compiler,
pieces of bytecode not generated by the compiler may easily violate these
conditions, and it seems implausible to obtain a precise analysis
without making any assumption on the structure of bytecode.
However, this should not be an issue in practice,
since the vast majority of transaction currently occurring in Ethereum blocks
looks like to call contracts with a verified Solidity source
7
7

7

Although we are not aware of any research explicitly quantifying
the fraction of Ethereum transactions directed to Solidity contracts,
some empirical evidence of this conjecture can be obtained
by inspecting blocks and their transactions in
https://etherscan.io/
,
which displays the Solidity code of target contracts.
According to
[
OHJ20
]
,
\sim
72% of all transactions
sent to contracts target contracts with verified source code.
.

A second simplification used in our tool is that the
over-approximation of the keys read/written by a transaction
does not exploit the transaction fields
(besides the called contract and function).
Thus, different calls to the same function but with different
actual parameters result in the same over-approximation.
Although this simplifies the implementation,
it may decrease the precision of the analysis,
because the values of the function parameters are left abstract.
Consequently, the occurrence net constructed by the tool
contains more dependencies than strictly needed.
For instance, the tool would not detect the swappable transactions
in the ERC-721 example described in Section
1.2
,
since there the transaction fields are essential to obtain a precise
over-approximation.
A possible improvement could be to refine the analysis tool
using all the transaction fields.

Finally, the measurements we performed in our experiment are too coarse-grained
to allow a precise estimation of the speed up achieved by running
the transactions in parallel.
For example, we did not consider the overhead required to maintain the threads
and to dispatch the transactions when executing the schedule given
by the occurrence net.
To precisely measure this overhead, one would need to
integrate our approach with an Ethereum node,
and use it to compute the achieved speed up.
Although preliminary, the results of our experiment
shown in
Table

1
are positive enough
to make us believe that a speed up will be confirmed
also when taking into account these overheads.

6.
Conclusions

We have proposed a theory of transaction parallelism for blockchains,
aimed at improving the performance of blockchain nodes.
We have started by introducing a general model of blockchain platforms,
and we have shown how to instantiate it
to Bitcoin and Ethereum, the two most widespread blockchains.
We have defined two transactions to be
swappable

when inverting their order does not affect the blockchain state.
Since swappability is undecidable in general,
we have introduced a static approximation, called strong swappability,
based on a static analysis of the observables
read/written by transactions.
We have rendered concurrent executions of a sequence of transactions as

step firing sequences
in the associated occurrence net.
Our main technical result, Theorem
16
, shows that
these concurrent executions are semantically equivalent to the
sequential one.
An initial experimental assessment of our approach in Ethereum
shows that there are margins to make it applicable in practice.

We remark that our work does not address the problem of
selecting and ordering transactions to maximize the gain of the miner,
i.e. it does not proposes strategies to construct blocks of transactions
(step
1
in the miner algorithm
described in Section
5
).
Rather, our theory studies how to exploit the available parallelism
to execute a block of transactions, assuming that the block is given
(which is always the case for validators).
Miners can follow different strategies to construct blocks,
driven by the economic incentives provided by the blockchain platform.
In Bitcoin, miner incentives are given by block rewards and
by the fees paid by users for each transaction included in a block.
In Ethereum, besides these incentives,
miners can extract value directly from smart contracts
by suitably ordering users’ transactions and inserting their own.
This form of
miner extractable value
has become prominent
with the emergence of DeFi contracts like
decentralized exchanges
[
DGK+20
,
QZG21
,
ZQC+21
]
.
Once a miner has formed a block of transaction according to its strategy,
our theory tells how to speed up its execution by parallelizing transactions.

In Ethereum, malevolent users could attempt
a denial-of-service attack by bloating the blockchain with
transactions directed to contracts which are hard to statically analyse.
This would make a naïve miner spend a lot of time executing
the static analysis on these adversarial transactions.
This kind of attacks can be mitigated by miner strategies which
put a strict upper bound to the execution time of the analysis.
Note that, since most transactions in Ethereum are directed to
a small number of well-known contracts,
like e.g. ERC tokens, DeFi contracts, etc.
[
OHJ20
]
,
to achieve an effective speed up it would be enough to
parallelize the transactions sent to these contracts,
and execute the transactions sent to unknown contracts
without any concurrency.

Aiming at minimality, our model does not include the
gas mechanism
,
which is used in Ethereum to pay miners for executing contracts.
The sender of a transaction deposits into it some crypto-currency,
to be paid to the miner which appends the transaction to the blockchain.
Each instruction executed by the miner consumes part of this deposit;
when the deposit reaches zero, the miner stops executing the transaction.
At this point, all the effects of the transaction
(except the payment to the miner) are rolled back.
Our transaction model could be easily extended with a gas mechanism,
by associating a cost to statements and recording the gas consumption in the environment.
Remarkably, adding gas does not invalidate approximations of read/written keys
which are correct while neglecting gas.
However, a gas-aware analysis may be more precise of a gas-oblivious one:
for instance, in the statement

{\tt{if}}\,{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{k}}}\,{\tt{then}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{long}}()};{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}\,{\tt{else}}\,{{{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{y}}}{\tt{:=}}{1}}

(where
{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{\mathtt{f}}}_{\color[rgb]{0.88,0.07,0.37}{long}}
is a function which exceeds the available gas)
a gas-aware analysis would be able to detect that
{\color[rgb]{1,0,1}{x}}
is not written.

Proof of Lemma

Direct from the fact that semantics of transactions is a function,
and it only depends on the blockchain states after the execution of

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}

\sigma

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
.

See
3

Proof A.1
.

By definition,
\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}
is the least equivalence
relation closed under the rules:

\begin{array}[]{c}\dfrac{}{{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}}{\scriptsize\textsc{[$\simeq$0]}}\quad\dfrac{}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}{\scriptsize\textsc{[$\simeq$1]}}\quad\dfrac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\rightleftarrows{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}{\scriptsize\textsc{[$\simeq$2]}}\quad\dfrac{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{0}}}}}}}\quad{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{1}}}}}}}}{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{0}}}}}}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{1}}}}}}}}{\scriptsize\textsc{[$\simeq$3]}}\end{array}

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\simeq_{\rightleftarrows}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
.
We have to show
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}
.
We proceed by induction on the rules above.
For rules
[
\simeq
0]
and
[
\simeq
1]
,
the thesis follows by reflexivity,
since
\sim
is an equivalence relation (Lemma
1
).
For rule
[
\simeq
2]
, the thesis follows immediately
by Definition
3
.
For rule
[
\simeq
3]
, first note that
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{0}}}}}}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{1}}}}}}}
. By the induction hypothesis it follows that:

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{0}}}}}}}\quad\text{and}\quad{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{1}}}}}}}

Therefore, by two applications of Lemma
2
:

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{1}}}}}}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{0}}}}}}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{1}}}}}}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}

Proof of Lemma
4

Item

1
.
For the case
\bullet=w
, let
{P}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
and

P\subseteq P^{\prime}
.
Let
Q
be such that
Q\cap P^{\prime}=\emptyset
.
We have to show that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
.
Since
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
,
it must be
Q\cap P=\emptyset
.
Then, since
{P}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,
it must be
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
, as required.
For the case
\bullet=r
, let
{P}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
and

P\subseteq P^{\prime}
.
We have to show that, for all
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
,
if
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\sim_{P^{\prime}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}
,
then
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{Q}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
. But this follows immediately
by the fact that
P\subseteq P^{\prime}
and
{P}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.

Item

2
.
Let
R
be such that
R\cap(P\cap Q)=\emptyset
.
Since

{P}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
and
(R\setminus P)\cap P=\emptyset
, then:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{R\setminus P}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}

Similarly, since
{Q}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
and

(R\setminus Q)\cap Q=\emptyset
, we have that:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\sim_{R\setminus Q}{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}

By assumption
R\cap(P\cap Q)=\emptyset
,
then
(R\setminus P)\cup(R\setminus Q)=R
.
By Definition
3
, we conclude:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\;\sim_{R}\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\;\sim_{(R\setminus P)\cup(R\setminus Q)}\;{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}

∎

Lemma 17
.

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\simeq_{\#}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}\;\;\implies\;\;{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\sim{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}

Proof A.2
.

Direct by Theorems
3
and
6
.

Appendix B
Proofs for
Section

4

Lemma 18
.

\oplus
is commutative and associative,
with
\lambda{p}.\bot
as neutral element.

Proof B.1
.

Trivial.

Lemma 19
.

If
\pi_{1}\oplus\pi_{2}=\pi
, then

\pi=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}
.

Proof B.2
.

Since
\pi_{1}\oplus\pi_{2}
is defined, it must be

\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{1}}\cap\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{2}}=\emptyset
.
Let
{p}
be an observable. We have two cases:

•

{p}\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi}
. Since
\operatorname{dom}{\pi}=\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{1}}\cup\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{2}}
, we have two subcases:

–

{p}\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{1}}
. Then,
\pi{p}=\pi_{1}{p}
.
By disjointness,
{p}\not\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{2}}
, and hence

\pi_{1}\pi_{2}{p}=\pi_{1}{p}
.

–

{p}\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{2}}
. Then,
\pi{p}=\pi_{2}{p}=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}{p}
.

•

{p}\not\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi}
.
Then,
{p}\not\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{1}}
,

{p}\not\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{2}}
,
and so
\pi{p}=\bot=\pi_{1}\pi_{2}{p}
.

Lemma 20
.

If
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
, then

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}\vartriangleleft({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}})
.

Proof B.3
.

By induction on
|{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}|
. For the base case, it must be

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}={\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
and hence
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}=\emptyset
. Then,

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}={\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}
.
Therefore, the thesis coincides with the first hypothesis.
For the induction case, it must be
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{2}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
, with

|{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{2}}}}}}}|=n
. Furthermore, it must be

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}=\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
, for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
such that

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{2}}}}}}}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}
. By the induction hypothesis:

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{2}}}}}}}\vartriangleleft({\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}})

Then:

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{2}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}={\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}\vartriangleleft(\{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}})={\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\cup{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}

Lemma 21
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
be such that

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}\implies{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
. Then, for all

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}\sim_{\mathtt{R}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}}$}

and
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket$}}$}
.

Proof B.4
.

A simple induction on
|{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}|
, using Definition
3

for the induction case.

We now formalize when a blockchain
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}

{defi}

[Serialization of multisets of transactions]

We define the relation
\vartriangleleft
between blockchains and multisets

\dfrac{}{{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}\vartriangleleft[]}\qquad\qquad\dfrac{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}}{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\vartriangleleft([{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}]+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}})}

Lemma 22
.

If
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}
for all
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}

Lemma 23
.

If
{{p}}\models^{r}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}\sim_{{p}}{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}

Proof B.6
.

Let
\pi_{1}=\Pi(\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket$},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})
and

\pi_{2}=\Pi(\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})
. By Definition
4
,

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket$}\pi_{1}

{p}\in Q
.
We have two cases:

•

{p}\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{2}}
.

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket$}\pi_{2}{p}=\pi_{2}{p}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}\pi_{2}{p}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket$}{p}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket$}{p}

•

{p}\not\in\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{2}}
.

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket$}\pi_{2}{p}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}}\rrbracket$}{p}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}{p}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{2}}}\rrbracket$}\pi_{2}{p}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket$}{p}

{defi}

Let
\Pi
be a state updater, and
let
\mathtt{W}
be such that
\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}:{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}\models^{w}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.
We say that
\Pi
and
\mathtt{W}
are
compatible
when

\forall\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}:\operatorname{dom}{\Pi(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})}\subseteq\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})
.

We extend the semantics of transactions
to finite
multisets
of transactions.
Hereafter, we denote with
[]
the empty multiset,
with
[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}}}]
the multiset containing

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{1}},\ldots,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{n}}
, and with

A+B
the sum between multisets,
i.e.
(A+B)(x)=A(x)+B(x)
for all
x
.

{defi}

[Semantics of multisets of transactions]

We denote the semantics of a multiset of transactions
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}
,
in a state
\sigma
and an update collector
\Pi
,
as
\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\sigma}
,
where the partial function

\llbracket{\cdot}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\sigma}
is defined as:

\;\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\sigma}$}\,=\,\sigma\bigoplus_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}}\Pi(\sigma,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})
.

Hereafter, we say that a multiset
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}
is strongly swappable
w.r.t. a relation
\mathcal{R}\subseteq\#
when:

\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}},\forall{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}-[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}]:\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}\,\mathcal{R}\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}

Lemma 24
.

Let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}
be strongly swappable w.r.t.
\#^{\mathtt{W}}_{\mathtt{R}}
,
let
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}
,
and let
\Pi
be compatible with
\mathtt{W}
.
Then, for all
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}
:
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}
.

Proof B.7
.

By induction on
|{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}|
. For the base case, it must be

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}=\emptyset
, and hence

\mbox{$\llbracket{\emptyset}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\mathbfsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\varepsilon}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}
.
For the induction case, it must be
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}={\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}
, with

|{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}|=n
.
Clearly,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}=[{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}]+{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}
for some
{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}
such that

{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}\vartriangleleft{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}
.
Let
\Pi(\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})=\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.
By the induction hypothesis:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}

(2)

Notice that:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}\pi^{\prime}

(3)

where
\pi^{\prime}\bigoplus_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}}\Pi(\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T^{\prime}}}))
.
Let
\Pi(\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})=\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}
.
Since

and
\Pi

is compatible with
\mathtt{W}
, it must be
\operatorname{dom}{\pi^{\prime}}\cap\operatorname{dom}{\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}}=\emptyset
, and hence
(\pi^{\prime}\oplus\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})

is defined.
Then, it must be:

\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}(\pi^{\prime}\oplus\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}\pi^{\prime}\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}

By Lemma
19

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}^{\prime}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}

By
Equation

\displaystyle=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}

By
Equation

2

(4)

We have that:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}\pi^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}

where
\pi^{\prime}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}=\Pi(\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{0}}}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$},{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}})
.

.
It remains to show that
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}\sim_{\mathtt{W}({{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}})}\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}

First notice that, by Lemmas

And hence:

\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf B^{\prime}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathrm{\textup{}}}}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\sf T}}}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}

(5)

The thesis
\mbox{$\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{\mathbb{T}}}}\rrbracket^{\Pi}_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}=\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}}\rrbracket_{\mbox{$\llbracket{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{B}}}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0.2,0.4}{0}}}\rrbracket$}}$}
then follows by

and
